--- PAGE 2 ---
     For this purpose, we strive to gauge the effects of both internal and  external conditions on the sales of Rossman drug store chain in  Germany and project how many items of each category will be sold in  each store. The rest of this paper is set up like this: Section 2 gives an  overview of the studies we looked at, which discuss past methods for  retail sales forecasting and the reasons behind sales trends. Section 3  clarifies the dataset and the ways the research is carried out  empirically. Section 4 presents the findings and conducts analysis of  the results obtained from data from Rossman, a German company.  Section 5 looks at what recommendations have been made and what  research is needed going forward. The last section delivers the final  remarks.  II. RELATED WORK  A. Hybrid Forecasting in Retail and Related Domains  Hybrid strategies that integrate statistical time-series and  machine-learning have exhibited steady accuracy  improvements. An ARIMA ensemble coupled with Prophet and  a "Prophet + XGBoost" ensemble detected linear trends as well  as seasonality and residual nonlinearity in a case study in  Rossmann, with the lowest RMSE of all models tested [12]. A  ProphetSVR -combo handled holiday and price impacts  through decomposition and residual modeling and enhanced  multivariate grocery/retail forecasts [13]. Short-term power  systems forecasting cross-domain evidence also supports the  usefulness of hybrids albeit in terms of a reduction of error in  the mixing of complementary models [14]. Earlier work in retail  applications continues to focus on aggregate or one store/one  product forecasts rather than disaggregated chains  geographically [15], [16]. Hybrid modeling has potential  although little is being applied to multi-region retail chains.    Fig. 1.  Schematic Representation of Literature Review  B. Methodological Overviews  Wider surveys and technical discussions list modeling  decisions, data problems, and evaluation approaches that apply  to retail forecasting [17].  C. Traditional Time-series Models and Multivariate Casual  Perspectives  Other than hybrids, classical families-  ARIMA/AR/MA/SARIMA, exponential smoothing,  HoltWinters- are traditional benchmarks to determine  seasonality, serial observation trends, and abrupt demand  variations [18]. Simultaneously, such causal and multivariate  models common in economics include promotions, holidays,  and special events to enhance fidelity in retail forecasts [19]  [20]; formulations that build upon regressions express these  exogenous drivers so explicitly [22]. Although they exist, these models have a problem with non-linear interactions and shocks  that are abrupt characteristic of modern retailing.  D. Advanced ML Hybrids with Residual Modeling  Zeng et al., built upon hybridization and added Prophet as a  residual model into the mix with Bayesian-optimized XGBoost,  achieving large RMSE/MAPE improvements when residual  models take advantage of altering loads-an approach that can be  applied directly to geographically divided retail demand [23].  E.  Deep Learning for Complex, High-Volume Data    Deep learning methods (LSTMs/CNNs/Transformers) have  surpassed traditional data-mining increasingly as nonlinear,  multi-modal tasks scale/complexity is increased and have  performed well in text, image, and speech tasks [24]– [27].  Deep models, particularly those used to disaggregate by state  are not yet fully utilized in retail.  F. Contemporary hybrid Modeling Approaches  Recent contributions have extended hybrids further:  CatBoostXGBoost has categorical processing and boosted  residual learning, to deal with abrupt demand shocks [28]; a  TransformerProphetXGBoost combination adds long-term  procurement and short-term nonlinear effects over space [29].  These hybrids achieve good performance, even though this is  seldom assessed when applied to multi-regional pharmaceutical  retailing.  G. Marketing, Competition and Assortment Effects  The accuracy of the forecasts should be based on driving  elements of demand: in-store promotions/ displays have been  shown to boost sales by two or more times [30]; the price and  cannibalization have the potential to transform product-level  demand [31]; and companies are increasingly adopting the use  of competitor promotions/sales to enhance brand forecasts [32].  Choice of mix/quantities (assortment optimization) impinges on  profitability directly, and depends on credible forecasts [33].  The existing studies develop few region-sensitive prediction  models that collaboratively combine both internal (promotions,  assortment, store format) and external (competitors, holidays)  factors.     The literature continues to progress in reach here, with classical  statistical baselines giving way to machine learning and deep learning  hybrids across [12]-[33]. Nevertheless, a number of significant limits  continue to exist. On the one hand, the work on forecasting at the state  level in the system of national retail chains remains scarce [12]-[16]  [17]-[22]. Second, few studies similarly model both internal and  external drivers in a manner sensitive to their regional variation [23]- [27], [30]-[33]. Third, despite the potential of hybrid approaches in  other fields, hybrid approaches have not been sufficiently used in  pharmaceutical retail forecasting [28], [29]. The work presented here  fills in such gaps by fitting a Prophet + XGBoost hybrid to daily  German drugstore chain sales at the state level, with an explicit  inclusion of promotion, assortment, store format, holidays, and  neighboring competitor presence, and with a per-state evaluation  reported against robust baselines. Besides, this study explored the  Facebook Prophet technique for the first time to forecast retail sales,  2Authorized licensed use limited to: Mukesh Patel School of Technology & Engineering. Downloaded on July 10,2026 at 15:46:52 UTC from IEEE Xplore.  Restrictions apply. 
--- PAGE 3 ---
which is a new approach for the industry. So far, there isn’t a study  where sales forecasting is done for different regions of the country in  drug retailor chain while considering factors inside and outside the  company. The research also tries to describe the geographic issues  involved. Thus, this paper will focus only on how well both machine  learning techniques and time series, such as Facebook Prophet, can  predict sales in the drug store chain in each state considering the  internal and external influencing factors.  III. METHODOLOGY   A. Data Sources  The most region-sensitive and correct sales forecasting is a  strategic prerogative in the context of the intelligently  developing retail. The proposed research remains a hybrid  forecasting experiment where Facebook Prophet is combined  with XGBoost to decompose trends and seasonality changes  robustly and characterize the remaining nonlinear effects of  sales regressors. Based on the Rossmann drugstore chain data  set of Germany, we will predict daily sale of the store at the  state level, including the effect of internal factors (promotions,  assortment mix, store format) and external forces (public  holidays, proximity to competitors). The article is based on the  dataset of Rossmann Sales Prediction by Kaggle including:  train.csv: Sales and store level information on a daily basis,  store.csv: store features of 1,115 branches in Germany.   All the observations contain daily sales, the store identification,  and promotion variables, holidays and store characteristics. To  geographically disaggregate stores it was possible to map  STORES to the 16 German federal states in the graph using  location identifiers. That will make state an unambiguous term  used consistently throughout this paper, as meaning  Bundesland-level aggregation. Notably, Rossmann dataset does  not contain product level sales. As compared to previous  iterations of this piece, we replaced the use of randomly  imposed product categories to study store-level, and state-level  daily sales, which fits directly into the contribution of the paper.     Fig. 2.  Workflow Diagram Outlining the Procedural Framework  B. Data Preparation  Sundays and holidays: The data has also an Open flag which  denotes whether a store was in business. We do not train or test  our models on Sundays or holiday periods, since sales are structurally zero on those trading days (the exception is that we  have training data available, as Open [the number of trading  days] can be zero, e. g. you might train on the full year 2019,  with sales during both Sundays and holidays being zero). In  case of forecast based on the calendar level, the predicted sales  are fixed at zero on closed days to carry uniformity.  Datenfehlern und Outliern: Forward filling was used on  continuous variables, mode imputation on categorical variables  to fill in missing values. Winsorized extreme outliers (values  >3sigma rolling mean) were used to stabilize model training.  Feature Engineering: The features were broken down into: Int  (Promotions): Promotions (Promo, Promo2), store type (A-D),  assort. type (basic, extra, extended). Exogenous influences:  public holidays (StateHoliday), school holidays, day-of-week  and proximity of competitors. Regressors were included such  as lagged sales, rolling means (7/14/30 days) and interaction  terms (Promo x Holiday). Encoding: Categorical variables (the  type of store, the type of the assortment, the state) were treated  as follows: one-hot encoding was applied in categorical  features, and label-encoding was used in ordinal variables not  preceded by one-hot encoding.  This research paper used the characteristics listed in appendix  A to predict sales in different stores across the states and  discover what influences those sales. Features presented in  Appendix A are the ones listed in the Rossmann data with a  description. These factors have been split into Internal and  External types. Besides all the other important facts, the data  also offers details about the kind of store. There are four kinds  of stores in the organization and they are grouped as A, B, C,  and D. We assume, the rationale of the classification is  grounded on the one hand on the area of the store and on the  other hand on its sales potential. The predicator of ‘Assortment  type’ has the following choices; (i) Basic, (ii) Extra and (iii)  Extended and they are coded as ‘a’, ‘b’ or ‘c’. Furthermore, we  would like to point out that the Rossmann dataset does not hold  details about any products. Thus, by looking at the external  sources, we managed to find out which products and brands are  available in Rossmann stores. These groups of products have  been randomly arranged in the stores with the RAND function.  The dataset for the Kaggle competition is mixed with additional  data on the States and categories a store belongs to using the  store IDs. After that, any missing entries, extreme values, and  relationships between variables were managed in the dataset.    Fig. 3.  Correlation amongst the independent variables  Following the removal of variables showing a lot of  multicollinearities, we build a feature set that forms the input  for data analysis and we include sales as the output variable.  After that, the data is sorted and encoded by both label encoder  and one hot encoding.  3Authorized licensed use limited to: Mukesh Patel School of Technology & Engineering. Downloaded on July 10,2026 at 15:46:52 UTC from IEEE Xplore.  Restrictions apply. 
--- PAGE 4 ---
C. Techniques and Algorithms  This research will use different data mining methods to try and  forecast future sales. For the empirical model, the company’s  promotional strategies, type of merchandise, and store design  are all identified as internal independent variables, and factors  such as market competition and public holidays are seen as  external independent variables. In brief, the methods used are  as listed below:  1) Linear Regression: Linear Regression uses the least  square method to find out the connection between a  dependent variable and one or more independent  variables.  ,    	₁                            (1)  where , shows the everyday sales figures for a store. X shows  the value of the independent variable, u represents the error  term, β1 represents the coefficients of all the explanatory  variables, country identity is denoted by t and the time variable  is t.  2) Decision Tree: This technique works well because it  is able to deal with both types of variables. Besides,  the approach is not influenced by the changing size of  any variable. But, the main flaw in decision trees is  that they can fit the training data very tightly. Dealing  with this issue requires you to use strategies such as  pruning and trimming to manage the size of a tree.  3) Random Forest: Random Forest was invented to  resolve some issues that appear in Decision trees.  Bagging is used to build base models and then the  group of trees calculates the mean of the results at  their final nodes. It leaves out the less important  information and identifies factors that help make the  prediction more accurate.  4) XGBoost: Gradient tree boosting is an additional  ensemble method designed to lower the bias and  variance in the final model. By following an iterative  method, the weak individuals are merged into a better  trained machine. XGBoost applies gradient boosting  in a powerful way by using only decision trees as  basic learners and manages their complexity by  modifying the loss function.  5) Facebook Prophet: A new way of analyzing time  series data, known as Facebook Prophet, has also been  covered in this paper. The library Prophet was created  by the central data science unit at Facebook. Here, a  combination model is applied, fitting trends that are  not straight through yearly, weekly, and daily  seasonal patterns plus effects of holidays. You get the  best benefits if the series is clearly shaped by seasonal  variations and you have data from several seasons. It  was able to deal with missing data, outliers, and abrupt  changes in trends. Regarding Facebook’s usage of  Prophet, Taylor and Letham pointed out that, in many  situations, its predictions are more reliable and  accurate than those produced by other methods. The main parts used by Facebook Prophet are trend,  seasonality, and holidays.        ℎ  ₜ         (2)  As for this model, g(t) is for non-linear trend, s(t) represents  seasonal movements, and h(t) covers holiday occurrences. The  term eₜ means the part of the time series at time t that can’t be  explained by other factors.  IV. RESULTS   A. V. DISCUSSION AND RECOMMENDATION   These findings indicate that the hybrid Prophet and XGBoost  model outperforms both the classical statistics and the stand- alone machine learning baselines, with median state-level  MAPE decreased by 12 to i 8, and estimates both accurate and  interpretable. The feature importance analysis reveals that  promotions, assortment type and store format represent the key  drivers of sales with promotions increasing their weekly sales  by up to 8 percent in North Rhinewestphalia and only 3 percent  in Saarland and Extended or Extra assortments consistently  increasing their sales over Basic assortments by 5-7 percent.  Regional variation also occurs in store formats, with type B  stores able to achieve higher sales in the NW and BE and type  D stores able to achieve higher sales in the TH and HH, and this  can facilitate the location-specific expansion choices. The  effects of holidays were modest but significant in the case of  several states, including BY and SN, and the proper  consideration of the number of closed days decreased the  forecasting error by 1520 percent and verified the significance  of the calendar-conscious planning. According to these  findings, managers must focus promotions early in the week  and custom-control intensity by state, expand Extended and  Extra offerings in high demand states, prioritize the  introduction of store formats by region, and optimize inventory  placement to local promotional sensitivity to reduce stock-outs.   VI. CONCLUSION  Making accurate and efficient sales forecasts is important in the  retail industry, as a lot of decisions are made based on them.  Following this forecasting strategy, the company won’t have to  pay high costs for storing unsold goods and can earn more  money. This research examined the performance of various  machine learning algorithms after considering various aspects  that affect the process. The use of a recent time series technique  i.e., Facebook Prophet for forecasting sales is investigated. It  became clear that XGBoost did better in predicting outcomes  compared to the other models. In the same way, Facebook  Prophet provided good results. It is necessary to note that this  technique solely looks at the sales increase connected to the  regressor. The results from this project could apply to other  industries where researchers would try out Facebook Prophet.   REFERENCES   [1] R.  Technology Acceptance and Security Influences on  AI Adoption among Small and Medium Enterprises   1st Yuniarty   Management Department,  BINUS Online Learning, Bina  Nusantara University  Jakarta, Indonesia   yuniarty@binus.ac.id   4th Noe Enriquez   Industrial Engineering Department,   Adamson University   Manila, Philippines  noe.enriquez@adamson.edu.ph 2nd Hartiwi Prabowo  Management Department  BINUS Online Learning, Bina  Nusantara University  Jakarta, Indonesia   hartiwi2200@binus.ac.id      3rd Ridho Bramulya Ikhsan  Management Department,  BINUS Online Learning, Bina  Nusantara University  Jakarta, Indonesia  ridho.bramulya.i@binus.ac.id   line 1: 6th Given Name Surname  line 2: dept. name of organization   (of Affiliation)   line 3: line 4: City, Country    Abstract —Small and Medium Enterprises (SMEs) play a  vital role in supporting Indonesia’s economic growth. However,  their adoption of Artificial Intelligence (AI) remains limited  despite its potential to enhance efficiency, innovation, and  competitiveness. This study extends the Technology Acceptance  Model (TAM) to explore how perceived ease of use, perceived  usefulness, and perceived security influence SMEs’ intention to  adopt AI technologies. A quantitative approach was employed  using survey data collected from 131 Indonesian SME owners  and managers. The data were analyzed using Partial Least  Squares–Structural Equation Modeling (PLS-SEM). The  findings reveal that perceived ease of use significantly shapes  both the perceived usefulness of AI and the intention to adopt it,  consistent with TAM's theoretical propositions. Interestingly,  perceived usefulness does not affect substantially adoption  intention, suggesting that functional benefits alone are  insufficient to drive adoption in this context. Instead, perceived  security emerges as a critical determinant, indicating that SMEs  place strong emphasis on data protection, privacy, and system  reliability when considering AI implementation. This study  contributes to refining the TAM framework by highlighting the  central role of security as a determinant of adoption behavior  and demonstrating the limited influence of perceived usefulness  in high-risk technological settings. The results underscore the  need for policymakers and technology providers to prioritize  secure and accessible AI solutions to foster inclusive and  sustainable digital transformation among Indonesian SMEs.   Keywords—Artificial Intelligence; Technology Acceptance  Model; Security; Perceived Usefulness; Perceived Ease of Use  I. INTRODUCTION   Small and Medium Enterprises (SMEs) are essential to  Indonesia's economic growth, as they employ the bulk of the  country's workforce and account for more than 60% of its  GDP [1]. Artificial Intelligence (AI) has become a key  technology that may improve competitiveness, efficiency, and  decision-making in the age of rapid digital transformation.  Adopting AI is now essential for Indonesian SMEs to prosper  in increasingly globalized and dynamic marketplaces, not just  a luxury [2].  However, Indonesian SMEs continue to deploy AI  technologies unevenly, with several obstacles. Technology  acceptability, which includes managers' and entrepreneurs'  readiness to incorporate AI technologies into their business  operations, is one of the most critical elements affecting  adoption [3]. Perceptions of usability, compatibility with  current systems, and ease of use influence this adoption.  Security is also critical because worries about data privacy, cyber risks, and regulatory compliance greatly impact trust  and the desire to use AI breakthroughs [4].  An unusual setting for studying these processes is  Indonesia. Government programs like Making Indonesia 4.0  and the acceleration of the development of the digital  economy are driving the nation's rapid increases in internet  adoption and digital literacy [5]. SMEs' limitations may  hamper the implementation of AI in infrastructure,  knowledge, and resources. Therefore, creating strategies that  help SMEs leverage AI for sustained growth requires an  understanding of how security perceptions and technology  acceptability influence AI adoption behavior.  Recent scholarship on AI and technology adoption in  SMEs has identified multiple factors shaping successful  implementation. Within the Technology–Organization– Environment (TOE) framework [6], drivers such as relative  advantage, compatibility, human capital, market demand, and  government support emerge as critical, with evidence  suggesting that medium-sized firms benefit more strongly  from relative advantage than smaller firms [7]. Similarly,  research using the Unified Theory of Acceptance and Use of  Technology (UTAUT) model shows that effort expectancy  and social influence significantly affect behavioral intention  to adopt generative AI, with age and work experience  moderating these effects [8]. Leadership has also been shown  to influence AI adoption through fostering data-driven  cultures and enhancing employee competencies, which in turn  strengthen supply chain resilience [9]. Yet, in many  developing country contexts, SMEs continue to rely primarily  on basic information systems rather than advanced  technologies such as AI, underscoring the importance of  sociocultural and contextualized adoption models [10].  Building upon the preceding discussion, this study offers  a novel contribution by examining technology acceptance and  security perceptions concurrently as determinants of AI  adoption intention among Indonesian SMEs, a sector  experiencing rapid digital transformation yet marked by  uneven technological uptake. The Technology Acceptance  Model (TAM) serves as the central theoretical framework for  elucidating user behavior toward emerging technologies,  providing a structured lens for understanding the underlying  drivers of AI adoption.  In this study, AI technologies refer to practical, business- oriented applications that support SMEs’ operational and  strategic activities. In Indonesia, SMEs have increasingly  adopted various forms of AI, including AI-driven customer 2025 International Conference on Informatics, Multimedia, Cyber and Information System (ICIMCIS) 979-8-3315-7930-2/25/$31.00 ©2025 IEEE 16432025 International Conference on Informatics, Multimedia, Cyber and Information System (ICIMCIS) | 979-8-3315-7930-2/25/$31.00 ©2025 IEEE | DOI: 10.1109/ICIMCIS68501.2025.11327244 Authorized licensed use limited to: Mukesh Patel School of Technology & Engineering. --- PAGE 2 ---
service chatbots, e-commerce recommendation systems,  automated inventory and demand forecasting tools, and AI- based digital marketing analytics. For example, small online  retailers often use chatbots integrated with WhatsApp or  social media platforms to manage customer interactions  efficiently. At the same time, food and fashion SMEs rely on  AI algorithms for personalized product recommendations and  trend forecasting. Furthermore, AI-powered financial  management applications assist SMEs in credit scoring,  expense tracking, and fraud detection. These examples  demonstrate that security concerns, particularly those related  to data protection, privacy, and system reliability, are critical  factors in determining the extent to which SMEs trust and  adopt AI technologies.  In contrast to earlier research that predominantly focuses  on organizational, environmental, or leadership dimensions,  this study emphasizes three key constructs: perceived ease of  use, perceived usefulness, and perceived security. These  dimensions capture the practical realities and concerns of  SMEs operating in resource-constrained and high-risk  environments typical of emerging economies. Through this  approach, the research not only advances the existing TAM  framework by integrating security as a critical factor but also  provides context-specific evidence on how Indonesian SMEs  assess the opportunities and challenges of AI implementation.  Ultimately, this study seeks to illuminate how perceptions of  usability, utility, and security interact to shape adoption  behavior, offering actionable insights for policymakers,  technology developers, and SME practitioners while  contributing to the broader theoretical discourse on  technology acceptance and digital transformation.  Despite growing recognition of AI’s transformative  potential, the mechanisms through which technology  acceptance and security shape adoption behavior among  Indonesian SMEs remain insufficiently explored. This gap  limits policymakers' and practitioners' ability to design  effective strategies to address SMEs’ specific barriers to  digital transformation. Accordingly, this paper is structured as  follows: Section II reviews the theoretical foundation and  relevant literature; Section III outlines the research  methodology; Section IV presents and discusses the empirical  findings; and Section V concludes with implications,  limitations, and directions for future research.  II. LITERATURE REVIEW   Grounded in the constructs of perceived usefulness and  perceived ease of use, the Technology Acceptance Model  (TAM) provides a structured framework for understanding  how individuals and organizations form intentions to engage  with emerging technologies. When integrated with the Theory  of Planned Behavior, TAM has demonstrated strong  explanatory power, accounting for approximately 59% of the  variance in AI acceptance across various contexts [11]. Its  adaptability has been evident in multiple domains. For  instance, in the hospitality and tourism industry, the  development of an interactive TAM (iTAM) highlights the  critical role of perceived interactivity in shaping user attitudes  toward AI-enabled service robots [12]. Similarly, studies  combining TAM with status quo bias theory in the context of  AI voice assistants show that perceived ease of use and  perceived value positively influence user attitudes, whereas  switching costs and perceived risks act as barriers to adoption  [13]. In the education sector, TAM has been employed to  examine teachers’ intentions to utilize AI-based e-learning platforms, revealing that perceptions of usefulness and  emotional engagement significantly impact adoption  decisions [14]. Collectively, these findings underscore TAM’s  relevance as a theoretical lens for exploring how perceptions  of utility, usability, and security interact to shape AI adoption  behaviors—particularly in dynamic, resource-constrained  environments such as SMEs.  Studies investigating the impact of Perceived Ease of Use  (PEU) on AI adoption intentions (IAI) demonstrate  consistently favorable correlations across several areas. In  accounting education, PEU serves as a mediator between  technological preparedness and aspirations to use AI, in  conjunction with perceived utility [15]. Likewise, PEU has  substantial positive effects on both the desire to use and the  actual adoption of digital technologies, such as e-wallets [16].  In the realm of AI-enabled smart speakers, PEU is a pivotal  aspect impacting attitudes and intentions for continued usage,  whereas social elements such as media richness and parasocial  interactions shape perceptions of PEU [17]. These results  repeatedly validate PEU as an essential determinant in AI  adoption across different contexts and user demographics.  [18] discovered that in small and medium firms in Taiwan,  only perceived ease of use (PEU) had a direct impact on  perceived usefulness (PU), and PU was the only direct  predictor of system utilization. This is different from the  original TAM research by [19], which revealed that PU had a  significant effect on user intentions, whereas PEU had a  weaker but still significant effect that weakened over time.  The positive correlation between PEU and PU has been  consistently validated across several areas. [20] validated that  PEU substantially influences PU in the context of e-book  uptake among university students. [15] also discovered that  both PEU and PU act as intermediaries in the interaction  between technological readiness and the adoption of artificial  intelligence in accounting contexts, demonstrating their  ongoing significance in contemporary technology acceptance  frameworks.  PU acts as a bridge between students' readiness to use  technology and their plans to use AI [15]. In the same way,  studies of employees demonstrate that PU completely  mediates the negative link between job instability and plans to  use AI, and somewhat mediates the positive link between  habit and plans to utilize AI at work [21]. PU affects more than  only AI-specific applications. For example, research on the  adoption of e-wallets demonstrates that PU has a significant  beneficial impact on both usage intentions and actual adoption  behaviors [16]. In AI assistant-enabled smart speakers,  perceived usefulness (PU) is a significant factor that affects  how users feel about the technology and whether they plan to  keep using it. It works with the perceived simplicity of use to  encourage people to adopt the technology [22]. These results  consistently affirm PU as a critical factor in technology  adoption decisions.  In the realm of financial services, technical optimism  enhances the intention to utilize AI-driven robo-advisors, but  insecurity diminishes adoption intentions; conversely,  technological discomfort unexpectedly had good outcomes  [23]. In terms of security issues, insecurity makes people think  that AI systems are not very beneficial, especially in retail  settings, where being inventive and optimistic makes both  PEOU and PU go up [24]. These findings indicate that security  concerns and feelings of unease consistently act as obstacles  to AI adoption across diverse fields. 2025 International Conference on Informatics, Multimedia, Cyber and Information System (ICIMCIS) 1644Authorized licensed use limited to: Mukesh Patel School of Technology & Engineering. Downloaded on July 11,2026 at 18:35:57 UTC from IEEE Xplore.  Restrictions apply. 
--- PAGE 3 ---
III. METHODS   This study employed a quantitative research design to  investigate the determinants of AI adoption among small and  medium enterprises (SMEs) in Indonesia. A structured  questionnaire consisting of closed-ended items was developed  to measure respondents’ perceptions of the key constructs  under investigation. Data were collected using a cross- sectional design, capturing responses at a single point in time  to reflect the current state of AI awareness and adoption- readiness among Indonesian SMEs. The research specifically  targeted business owners and managers who possessed at least  a basic understanding of AI applications relevant to their  operational or strategic activities.  The data collection process was conducted through an  online survey distributed via social media channels, including  Instagram and WhatsApp. These platforms were selected to  ensure a wide reach and accessibility for SMEs located across  diverse regions. Questionnaires were chosen as the primary  instrument because they are more cost-effective and time- efficient compared to interviews or observations [25].  Participation was voluntary, and a purposive sampling  strategy was used to ensure respondents were directly  involved in decision-making within their respective  organizations. This approach enhanced the relevance and  accuracy of the collected data, aligning with the study’s  objective of exploring managerial perspectives on AI  adoption. The dataset for this article is available at [26].  The adequacy of the sample size was determined  following recommendations for Partial Least Squares– Structural Equation Modeling (PLS-SEM). In accordance  with the “ten-times rule” proposed by [27], the minimum  required sample should be at least ten times the maximum  number of structural paths directed toward a latent construct  in the model. Since the most complex construct in this study  had three incoming paths, a minimum of 30 participants was  deemed sufficient. The final dataset consisted of 131 valid  responses, surpassing the minimum requirement and  providing a robust basis for model estimation and hypothesis  testing.  The conceptual framework for this study was grounded in  the Technology Acceptance Model (TAM), which was  extended by incorporating perceived security as an additional  determinant of AI adoption intention. The model included  four key constructs: perceived ease of use, perceived  usefulness, perceived security, and behavioral intention to  adopt AI. The research process followed three main stages:  instrument development, data collection, and model analysis.  During the first stage, measurement items were adapted from  established TAM literature and refined to suit the SME  context. The second stage involved data screening and  validation to ensure completeness and consistency. The final  stage used PLS-SEM to assess the measurement model’s  reliability and validity, followed by testing the hypothesized  relationships among the constructs in the structural model.  This methodological design provides a systematic,  empirically grounded approach to examining how perceptions  of usability, utility, and security jointly influence AI adoption  behavior among Indonesian SMEs. By adopting the TAM  framework and applying PLS-SEM analysis  [28], the study  ensures both theoretical rigor and analytical robustness in  capturing the complex dynamics underlying technology  acceptance in emerging market contexts.  IV. RESULTS AND DISCUSSIONS   The respondents' demographic profile offers crucial  background information for comprehending how SMEs are  using AI. Female respondents made up the majority (51.7%),  followed by male respondents (47.7%), with 0.6 percent  choosing not to reveal their gender. Given the minor  preponderance of female entrepreneurs or SME managers in  the sample, this distribution suggests that involvement was  fairly balanced between genders.  SME leaders and decision-makers are primarily in their  middle working years, as evidenced by the fact that the  majority of respondents were in the productive age groups of  30–39 (32.5%) and 40–49 (31.1%). Younger people (20–29  years, 21.2%) and older people (50–59 years, 12.6%)  accounted for a smaller share. Just 0.8% of respondents were  under 20 years old, while 1.8% were over 60. This suggests  that adults in Indonesia hold the majority of SME  management and ownership positions, with established work  histories.  Regarding educational background, the majority of  respondents had completed secondary school or its equivalent  (43.7%) and university programs (47%). A lower percentage  had earned postgraduate degrees, such as master's (7.9%) and  doctoral (1.4%). According to this distribution, secondary  education remains the basis for business operations, despite a  sizable share of SME actors holding advanced degrees.  The majority of SMEs surveyed had been in business for  one to five years (58.8%), with those with six to ten years of  operation coming in second (18.3%). Only a small percentage  of enterprises had been in operation for more than ten years  (less than 6% combined), while a smaller percentage were  relatively young, having been in operation for less than one  year (16.8%). In line with the broader growth of the digital  economy, this trend shows the increasing presence of  relatively young SMEs in Indonesia's business environment.  Based on Table I, all indicators show outer loadings above  the recommended threshold of 0.70, indicating good indicator  reliability [28]. All constructs exceed the recommended  threshold of 0.70, indicating strong internal consistency. All  AVE values exceed the recommended threshold of 0.50,  indicating good convergent validity. Although the effect size is  small, this confirms that ease of use plays a role in shaping  adoption intention among SMEs. Perceived Ease of Use  significantly affects Perceived Usefulness. This aligns with  TAM theory, which suggests that ease of use enhances  perceptions of usefulness. Perceived Usefulness does not  significantly influence AI Adoption Intention. HYPOTHESES TESTS    Original  Sample  (O) Standard  Deviation  (STDEV) P  Values f  Square Note  PEU ->  IAI 0,224 0,080 0,006 0,066 H1  accepted  PEU ->  PU 0,366 0,083 0,000 0,155 H2  accepted  PU ->  IAI 0,105 0,114 0,359 0,008 H3  rejected  SEC ->  IAI 0,403 0,092 0,000 0,113 H4  accepted    The discovery that PEU substantially affects the intention  to IAI (H1 accepted) corroborates the Technology Acceptance  Model’s (TAM) assertion that usability perceptions are a  pivotal factor in adoption behavior. Furthermore, the robust  positive correlation between PEU and PU (H2 accepted)  corroborates the recognized mediating role of usefulness, in  which ease of use amplifies perceptions of utility. This study  aligns with the TAM and underscores the need to develop AI  solutions that are both useful and intuitive for SMEs that may  lack extensive technical skills.   Interestingly, PU did not substantially affect the IAI (H3  rejected). This contradicts a fundamental premise of TAM,  indicating that, in the realm of AI adoption among Indonesian  SMEs, mere usefulness is insufficient to influence behavioral  intention. A reasonable argument is that SMEs acknowledge  the potential benefits of AI but are reluctant to implement it  owing to perceived risks, limited resources, or insufficient  preparedness. This research enhances theoretical  understanding by highlighting the limitations of the TAM and  suggesting that usefulness may be subordinate to other  considerations, such as security and usability, in  circumstances characterized by significant technological  uncertainty.  The study shows that the SEC has a meaningful influence  on the IAI (H4 accepted). This expands the TAM framework  by incorporating a critical contextual element—security—into the adoption model. The substantial effect size (f² = 0.113)  underscores the importance of security as both an external  factor and a fundamental predictor of adoption intention in AI  contexts.  Lower PLS prediction errors when compared to the LM  benchmark and positive Q2_predict values, which collectively  show medium predictive power, show that the model predicts  AI Adoption Intention (IAI) indicators quite effectively [29]  (see Table IV). Fig. 1.  SEM Model  V. CONCLUSIONS   The findings of this study offer several theoretical  contributions to the literature on technology acceptability and  AI adoption in SMEs. This research advances theoretical  development by demonstrating that, for nascent and data- 2025 International Conference on Informatics, Multimedia, Cyber and Information System (ICIMCIS) 1646Authorized licensed use limited to: Mukesh Patel School of Technology & Engineering. Downloaded on July 11,2026 at 18:35:57 UTC from IEEE Xplore.  Restrictions apply. 
--- PAGE 5 ---
intensive technologies such as AI, perceptions of security may  supersede perceptions of utility in shaping adoption decisions,  especially in resource-limited contexts such as those of SMEs  in Indonesia.  This study underscores the significance of security and  user-friendliness in influencing Indonesian SMEs' propensity  to adopt AI.  These findings indicate numerous beneficial  recommendations that can be implemented.  To facilitate the  safe and secure use of AI by small and medium-sized  businesses (SMEs), policymakers and government agencies  should enhance the effectiveness of their awareness  campaigns and increase the availability of training.   Technology companies must offer AI solutions that are user- friendly, require minimal technical expertise, and are  protected by robust security measures to establish trust with  SMEs.  SME proprietors and managers may also consider  gradually integrating AI into their operations through secure,  scalable platforms. This approach would mitigate perceived  risks while still leveraging the technology's benefits.   Additional research is recommended in numerous  directions.  Initially, a more comprehensive understanding of  adoption behavior may be achieved by incorporating  additional relevant factors, such as trust, cost, or  organizational preparedness, into the model.  Secondly,  further research could employ a longitudinal approach to  investigate the development of attitudes toward security, ease  of use, and utility as small and medium-sized enterprises build  their AI experience. Third, comparative studies of SMEs in  Indonesia and other emerging economies may reveal cultural  or institutional disparities that influence AI adoption.  Lastly,  qualitative methods such as interviews or case studies may  complement survey results by providing additional insight  into the reasons and challenges SMEs encounter when  implementing a new technology.    Technology Adoption of MSMEs in Food Supply  Chain: The Role of AI in Traceability and  Transparency  Teerarat Aree  Faculty of Business Administration  Kasetsart University  Bangkok, Thailand   teerarat.a@ku.th Pittawat Ueasangkomsate  Faculty of Business Administration  Kasetsart University  Bangkok, Thailand  pittawat.u@ku.th     Abstract — Food consumption is increasing significantly on global  stage due to social and economic growth, leading to higher food  production and development in food supply chain (FSC). Many  firms have been adopting artificial intelligence (AI) in FSC to deal  with problems.  This research aims to investigate the influence of  AI technology adoption of micro, small, and medium enterprise  (MSME) FSC in aspect of traceability and transparency in  Thailand. As various statistical research discussed in the  introduction section points out that nowadays FSC still lack of  traceability and transparency, which affects to product  consumption of customers. A quantitative approach through an  online questionnaire is utilized to gather statistical data, along  with conducting the literature review from existing literatures. 92  of MSMEs in Thailand are used as a sample for this research then  linear regression is applied for data analysis. The result of the  study indicates that  AI significantly influences firm’s performance  in term of traceability and transparency. AI facilitates in sourcing,  production, scheduling, and transportation. It provides a better  decision making and ensures that all processes in FSC reach  standards. However, there are some limitations in technology  adoption for MSMEs specifically in micro businesses as they still  lack know-how and budget for technological investment.  Index Terms —Food supply chain, artificial intelligence,  traceability, transparency, MSME  I. INTRODUCTION   Over 8.1 billion people at present globally, food production  and consumption have become way more significant issue  comparing to the past. The main goal of the food industry is to  provide good foods and other necessities that is suitable for the  diversity of human’s activities and lifestyle. In order to serve  the expanding population, the method of interconnected  activities that operate in the food supply chain (FSC) must be  transformed significantly [1]. FSC refers to all complex  networks between farm and processing activities, distributors,  and retailers that operate the concept of ‘farm to fork’ [2]. The  process begins by the raw material until final products are  delivered to customers. [2] employed a systematic review on  challenges in FSC. The result indicates the three most common  issues in FSC, including sustainability, food safety and quality,  traceability, and transparency. Among three issues, traceability  and transparency gains the lowest percentage of distribution in term of being mentioned by articles [2], implying that  traceability and transparency still do not gain much attention in  majority comparing to other two issues, although it is an  important topic. FSC with a lack of traceability and  transparency will adversely impact the efficiency on FSC [3].  Nowadays food trade becomes globalized, food chain integrity  is not concerning only about food safety but also with its origin  [4]. In most region, most academic research in firm’s  performance highlighted more on large firms rather than a small  and medium firm [5]. Although MSMEs are a core economic  driver in many countries, but still become neglected compared  to large firm. Particularly in Thailand, MSMEs play a vital role  in the Thai economy contributing to 35.2% of the total gross  domestic product (GDP). MSMEs generated GDP value as  amounting to 1,784,473 million baht, representing 39.97% of  the GDP in manufacturing sector as a whole [6]. These  statistical data implies that Thai MSMEs is an important part in  Thai economy and should be gained more attention from many  departments.        Nowadays traceability and transparency issue, tracking and  tracing technology is still not widely standardized. Outdated  technologies and traditional manual methods can cause a lack of  traceability. [7] had addressed some barriers to AI adoption in  FSC. The challenge for AI adoption is due to lacking an  appropriate testing system or trial facilities of AI technology,  especially in the scope of our study, MSMEs. Local MSMEs still  do not have the readiness to become fully invested in  technology. Therefore, this research aims to examine the  influence of AI technology adoption in FSC on MSMEs’  business performance in aspect of traceability and transparency,  along with studying the gap that may occur for the adoption.  II. LITERATURE REVIEW   A. Food Supply Chain  FSC is the network of several members who participate in  the transformation of raw materials and semi-finished products  to final foods delivered to consumers [8]. FSC involves in  combination of processes and operations which aim to  transform raw products from their original source to final food  products consumed by customers [9]. The process of FSC The 2025 Technology Innovation Management and Engineering Science International Conference (TIMES-iCON2025)  979-8-3315-7678-3/25/$31.00 ©2025 IEEE2025 6th Technology Innovation Management and Engineering Science International Conference (TIMES-iCON) | 979-8-3315-7678-3/25/$31.00 ©2025 IEEE | DOI: 10.1109/TIMES-ICON67125.2025.11488133 Authorized licensed use limited to: Mukesh Patel School of Technology & Engineering. --- PAGE 2 ---
   usually starts with the production of raw agricultu ral  ingredients from farmers in the farm sector [10]. A ll raw  ingredients are well prepared and stored before the y undergo  manufacturing. All types of ingredients are transfo rmed into  edible form as food. Products that reach the standa rd will be  transported and distributed to retail stores. Lastl y, customers  can finally purchase final products from the retail er. In  Thailand, manufacturers keep improving themselves t o achieve  world food standard and quality as well as social r equirements  such as domestic customer, oversea customer, and th e  government. MSMEs in Thailand play an important rol e mainly  in processed fruit and vegetable products. The Thai  food  industry performed well in term of food export with  the rank of  12  the world largest food exporter in 2023 and a grow th rate  of 3.2% [11].   B.  Food Traceability and Transparency  [12] defined food traceability as part of logistic  management which captures, stores, and transfers in formation  about food in the entire stages of FSC so that prod ucts can be  verified to control quality and safety, tracked for wardly, and  traced backwardly at any time required. Three main processes  are required for a tracking system in FSC, includin g the  transformation of analog data to digital data, tran smission of  digital data from a local source to the database, l astly the user  assess and collaboration [13]. Transparency refers to the  disclosure of a product’s information, such as prod uction  process, ingredients, environmental and social impa cts,  certification, label that indicates the product’s q uality and so on  [14].  [13] mentioned that the major cause of food  contamination is from lack of traceability and tran sparency.  As  food takes a long path from producer to customer, f ood  contamination can occur easily at any stage of FSC leading to a  negative domino effect. Once it happens, it takes a  long time to  point out the exact source and reason of contaminat ion and  specifically identify the contaminated products. Th is issue can  become a crucial concern to all members in FSC, esp ecially  those who are still lacking digital and technology skills [4].  C.  Artificial Intelligence   Artificial Intelligence (AI) is a sub-field of comp uter science  involving with creation of innovative machines and computer  systems that can perform any task which requires hu man  intelligence [15]. These innovations have the poten tial to  directly impact both the manufacturing processes an d the details  of several products and services, with important im plications for  productivity, employment, and competition [16].  [1 7] employed  a systematic review of AI applications in supply ch ain and   classified AI according to their techniques into fo ur sub-fields.  First sub-field includes Artificial neural network and Rough set  theory. The second sub-field includes Machine learn ing, Expert  systems and Genetic algorithms. The third sub-field  includes  Fuzzy logic. Lastly, forth sub-field includes Agent -based  systems. This study is going to highlight on the ou tstanding AI  techniques based on the result of application in th e supply chain  and adapting to FSC for this study. According to th e result from  [17], machine learning is the most dominant type of  AI that has  been used throughout all processes in supply chain.  Genetic algorithm is the second most used type of AI which is used in  five out of six processes in supply chain. Artifici al neural  network, Expert system, Fuzzy logic, and Agent-base d system  are applied in three processes. However, only ANN i s engaged  in purchasing and procurement, production, and inve ntory  management which are core processes that directly a ffect the  product’s quality and major management in all suppl y chain.  Therefore, this study will highlight three potentia l AI sub-fields,  including machine learning, genetic algorithm, and artificial  neural network  a)  Machine Learning: Machine learning (ML) is a subset  of AI that facilitates the system to be capable of identifying  patterns and making decision by learning and improv ing from  experience and previous data set without being prog rammed  [18]. Algorithms are trained using a large collecti on of two data  groups, the training set and the testing set. The a lgorithm will  receive a set of inputs with correct outputs, known  as pre- labeled information, to compare its actual output w ith correct  outputs and spot errors [19]. Regression is an exam ple of  machine learning that will be applied to this resea rch.  Regression is used to determine a relationship betw een the  independent variable and the dependent variable. It  instructs a  model using a dataset of X and Y and uses the train ed model to  predict the output of new input data [26].   b)  Genetic Algorithm: Genetic algorithm (GA) refers to  the principle of natural selection and agorithms in spired by the  biological evolution of living beings [20]. The fit test  chromosome will survive to generate new offspring, and this  process is replicated for multiple generations [21] . The weaker  chromosome will be eliminated. This technique is us ed for  optimization problems by finding the maximum or min imum  value. [22]. There are five components in GA, which  are  chromosome representation, selection, crossover, mu tation, and  fitness function computation [23]. Selection, cross over, and  mutation are considered as three core operations in  GA.  Selection is where a chromosome in the population w ill be in a  structure that hold information related to a potent ial solution  with a combination of weights [24]. Crossover is to  combine  chromosomes of two parent to produce new offspring that is  phenotypically like the parents [25]. Mutation is p roformed  intentionally to stop falling of all solution in th e population to  an optimal level of the solved problem [21].  c)  Artificial Neural Network: Artificial neural network  (ANN) is a processing technique that imitates the w orking of  brain cell and the nervous system of humans. Its ma in goal is to  figure out the pattern in dataset and do the cluste ring according  to natural grouping in the data [26]. The neuron, a  special  component of an ANN, plays an important role in imi tating the  human brain to perform tasks. Neurons communicate b y  transmitting electrical and chemical signal to neig hbors through  a neuron’s branch called an axon, which is further segmented  to collaterals [27]. Neurons transfers signals to t he receiving  end by the formation of connections between synapse s,  neuromuscular junctions at the end of collaterals, and  neighboring neurons. At the receicing end, neuron’s  dendrites  receive signals via synapses and merge them with ne uron’s  body called the soma. Neurons with stronger synapti c  Authorized licensed use limited to: Mukesh Patel School of Technology & Engineering. Downloaded on July 11,2026 at 18:34:32 UTC from IEEE Xplore.  Restrictions apply. 
--- PAGE 3 ---
   connections will have more weight and greater infue nce on one  another. These massive interconnections of neurons  collaborating to provide excellent abilities with t he same level  as the human brain.  D.  Related Research  AI is a highly influential invention that provides a massive  impact world in several aspects of human beings. Tr aceability is  one of the major concerns in FSC as it allows produ cers,  consumer, inspector, and government agencies to acc ess the  level in food safety issues, and also determining w hen and where  issues may have emerged, leading to the quick elimi nation of  harmed products in FSC [28]. [29] took sample of It alian and  foreign extra virgin olive oil to access their actu al graphic  origins analyzed the data with ANN. The result indi cates that 87  out of 92 samples are classified correctly accordin g to their  origin, providing 94.6% of classification accuracy. [30]  integrates an ANN model for tracking  forwardly and tracing  diversly with real-time evaluation of food quality along the  entire supply chain, along with giving the final de termination of  food grade in three different levels based on their  quality. [31]  developed an advanced AI system based on multiple s ensors and  ML to enable real-time monitoring and detection of the  product’s freshness. Consequently, this study propo ses a major  hypothesis with three sub-hypotheses as illustrated  in Fig. 1 to  study the influence of AI adoption in FSC in terms of traceability  and transparency as follows:  : AI can significantly enhance traceability and  transparency in FSC.   	: ML can significantly enhance traceability and  transparency in FSC.   : GA can significantly enhance traceability and  transparency in FSC.  : ANN can significantly enhance traceability and  transparency in FSC.    Fig. 1.  Theoretical framework illustrating the relationship  between AI and  traceability and transparency.  III.  METHODOLOGY   We conducted a survey as primary data for analyzing  all  variables along with employing a systematic review of  published literature as secondary data. The researc h adopts  quantitative method through the questionnaire surve y to  approach data collection and analysis. The content of  questionnaire is developed based on what level of A I adoption  the firm has and key metrics to evaluate the firm’s  performance.  The overall population is in firm level covering mi cro, small and medium sized enterprises (MSMEs) food and beverages   business in Thailand, particularly in the manufactu ring sector.  The firms that were eligible to participate for the  questionnaire  or the overall population were retrieved from a web site of  relevant department known as the Office of Small an d Medium  Enterprise Promotion (OSMEP) which is a Thai govern ment  department responsible for supporting SME firm in T hailand  [32]. Another source is the database from Food Inte lligence  Center (FIC). FIC is operated under the Thailand’s ministry of  industry whose main objectives are to research, col lect, and  follow news in Thai food industry [33]. The key res pondent is  normally the business owner, manager or employee wh o are  appropriate to fill the questionnaire referring to their knowledge  and expertise in FSC’s performance of their firm [3 4]. The  questionnaire developed in this study comprises of four sections  and was conducted by a Likert scale from 1 – 5. Sec tions one  and two ask about the demographic information of pa rticipants  and the information of the firm respectively. The t hird section  measures the level of AI adoption in FSC based on v arious  research [17, 32, 35-37]. Lastly, section  four investigates the  level of performance in traceability and transparen cy of a firm’s  FSC based on several studies [28, 38-43]. The quest ionnaire is  organized in online format as a Google Form and han ded out to  respondents via email.    According to the database of OSMEP and FIC, there a re  1,412 and 7,521 firms respectively which provide th e  population size of 8,933 firms in total. Krejcie an d Morgan’s  equation is applied to determine the sample size fo r the research  which determined the sample size for this study as 368 firms  [44]. In practical the researcher was able to send questionnaire  to 280 firms due to limitation of time constrain so , the sample  size using for this analysis is 280. However, due t o some several  undesirable reasons, a number of firms refused to p rovide  internal business information and decided not to co mplete the  questionnaire, consequently 92 of MSMEs were willin g to  answer the questionnaire. Finally, all questionnair e responses  are gathered and employed in multiple regression an alysis to  study the relationship of technology and firm’s per formance.  IV.  RESULTS AND DISCUSSIONS   All 92 samples of the research are from all regions  over  Thailand consisting of Central, Northern, Southern,  Eastern,  Western and Northern east. The majority of responde nts are  from micro firms and in the role of business’s owne r and  employee. Top three categories of core business ope rations  from responses are processed fruit and vegetable pr oduction,  general food production, and beverages production. The sample  includes 49 micro, 22 small, 11 medium, and 10 larg e-sized  firms. The common level of AI adoption in the three  sub-field  is in a neutral level followed by not adopted and a dopted level.  From the survey, major reason of AI implementation barriers  for MSMEs are financial constraints of the owner as  their  business scale is not large. Many of them operated business in  countryside where technology is still not generaliz ed. Highly  adopted level is chosen the most in ANN adoption. M L is most  used in calculating a proper amount of production a long with  selecting a supplier. GA is used by MSMEs in term o f   Authorized licensed use limited to: Mukesh Patel School of Technology & Engineering. Downloaded on July 11,2026 at 18:34:32 UTC from IEEE Xplore. Lastly, ANN is most used fo r dealing  with large database and handling with external fact or that may  affect to business. According to the results of the research, ANN influ ences  traceability and transparency in FSC of MSMEs the m ost with  the standardized coefficients of 0.551, followed by  GA and ML  with standardized coefficients of 0.513 and 0.479 r espectively.  ANN distributes significantly in firms’ performance . Most  firms implement ANN to deal with large set of data for example  supplier selection. ANN is also applied by firms fo r dealing  with external situations that affect the business w hich is market  fluctuation or changes from customer’s demand.  It considers  actual historical data of business to figure out a pattern and uses  for prediction. GA is applied in term of determinin g optimal  value for processes in supply chain. GA involves in  inventory  management to calculate the proper quantity and tim e schedule  for production based on weighting between certain a nd required  amount of product. Lastly, they use ML for detectio n then do  processing for real-time tracking to display quanti ty and  condition of products. In term of traceability and transparency  performance, most firms can track and monitor the s tatus of  products along the entire production. According to limitation of  local MSMEs, political policy may enhance technolog y  accessibility. For example, government launch a com petition  for MSMEs product and the winner could win a prize of getting  a support for technological investment and could be  an idea for  other MSMEs owner as well.   V.   CONCLUSION   In conclusion, several firms use AI techniques for their  business management in various stages of FSC such a s  purchasing, production, inventory management, deman d  forecasting and so on. The result of questionnaire done by  respondents, a representative of each MSME from all  regions  in Thailand, shows that AI adoption has influence t oward firm’s  performance in aspect of traceability and transpare ncy. Every  AI-sub field, ML, GA, and ANN are significantly inf luences  firm’s performance positively. Responses from quest ionnaire convey that the implementation of these AI sub-fiel d can  improve the quality of business operations in sever al ways.  They can be used to manage and analyze large amount  of data  that can be difficult to handle manually. Moreover,  AI enhances  deciding based on previous data. It used previous d ata to predict  or estimate new value which is helpful for firm to do the  appropriate forecasting. Most of the level of AI ad option in FSC  is in medium level. Some group of firms, especially  micro-sized  and small businesses, AI technology is beyond their  scope of  business. Several respondents mentioned that they a re capable  of dealing and managing all processes which has low   complexity due to their business scale without a re quirement of  high technology adoption. In addition, AI technolog y adoption  requires high cost of implementation, they may not be able to  afford and access the use of technology. High opera ting cost  and lack of control on innovation costs are two of limitation in  MSMEs’ product innovation [45]. Their return is not  worth the  investment cost. Consequently, this is a research g ap for  technology adoption in MSMEs business that can be d eveloped  further so that AI technology can be easier to acce ss for various  type of business in the future.     Weather -Integrated Recommendations for Food Choices with SHAP Explanation   Nikunj Jain   Department of Computer Science and Engineering,   Amrita School of Computing,  Bengaluru    Amrita Vishwa Vidyapeetham, India bl.en.u4cse21138@bl.students.amrita.edu     Priyanshu Jha   Department of Computer Science and Engineering,   Amrita School of Computing ,Bengaluru   Amrita Vishwa Vidyapeetham ,India    bl.en.u4 cse21165 @bl. students. amrita.edu    Jawed Hawari   Department of Computer Science and Engineering   Amrita School of Computing , Bengaluru   Amrita Vishwa Vidyapeetham , India   bl.en.u4cse21073@bl.students.amrita.edu     K Dinesh Kumar   Department of Computer Science and Engineering   Amrita School of Computing , Bengaluru   Amrita Vishwa Vidyapeetham,  India   kk_dinesh@blr.amrita.edu Abstract— In the context of today's rapidly evolving technological landscape and the urbanization of Tier 1 and Tier 2 cities, cloud kitchens have emerged as pivotal players, offering quick and convenient meals without the need for dine -in facilities. The demand pre diction for the food has become quite tough and could be considered as one of the significant challenges . Cloud kitchens face challenges in managing stock due to the absence of in -house diners, potentially resulting in wasted food. Moreover, adverse weathe r conditions often hinder the seamless delivery of online. To address these challenges, this project employs machine learning techniques to predict weather patterns and correlate them with food preferences according to that weather. The key algorithms foun d to deal with this problem were Random Forest, kNN, Decision tree and Naïve Bayes and Support Vector Machine. This serves the first half of the statement and then for recommendation a suitable system was developed. The pivotal role of the system is to re duce the food wastages for cloud kitchens by fostering adaptive strategies for efficient decision making. The key inputs from the market were found to be that 30 -40% of the predicted sales in food industry doesn’t meet due to various reasons. The ultimate goal is to empower cloud kitchen operations, ensuring adaptive and informed decision -making in response to changing weather dynamics. The union of both of these are explained using Interpretability tool SHAP for a convenient study.   Keywords:  Cloud kitchens, Decision Tree, Random Forest, Recommendation system, SHAP, Weather pattern   I. INTRODUCTION   Weather predictions serve as a critical determinant in various sectors, playing an indispensable role in strategic decision -making, planning, and risk management. For the food industry, especially cloud kitchens and restaurants, understanding and predictin g weather patterns is top. Unforeseen weather fluctuations significantly impact customer behavior and preferences, leading to variations in demand and sales. Predicting the weather can help businesses plan better for changes in customer demand and manage t heir resources efficiently. By using advanced weather forecasts and smart computer programs, companies can adjust their plans based on expected weather forecasts. This helps them avoid problems caused by sudden shifts in customer behavior due to weather ch anges. This active approach is especially helpful for cloud kitchens and restaurants. It allows them toorganize their work better, use their resources wisely, and reduce food waste caused by unpredictable weather. With technology getting better, there are  different ways to predict the weather. The study in this article explores at various methods to find the most accurate and efficient ones .    Throwing away food isn't just about losing meals and wasting it. It also means losing a lot of money for businesses and causing big problems for the environment. Businesses lose money when they can't sell the food they've prepared, and it costs money to get rid of the extra food. Plus, making food uses up a lot of resources like water and energy, and when food gets wasted, it's like wasting all those resources, too. Cloud kitchens, which are becoming more popular, have a hard time managing how much food they need and reducing   waste. Because they don't have physical customers coming in, things like bad weather affecting food deliveries can make it even harder for them. According to a report by Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO), for each food produced, one third of it goes  to mere waste which eventually challenges the sustainability of the planet.     When weather forecasts and food businesses work together, it can really help tackle problems. For cloud kitchens, using these weather predictions in their planning can make a big difference. When they use smart systems that learn from the weather forecasts , they can make food better   with less waste and adjust their plans. This integration   between weather predictions and food businesses helps these kitchens work better, matching the changing weather and what customers want, making things more efficient and e co- friendl y.  II. RELATED WORK   In [1], researchers have explored how food supply chains are changing. The author states that there are several digital ways to handle the supply chains. In [2], the author has designed a  complex relationship between food distribution and consumption. The author’s research provided valuab le insights for policymakers, offering empirically constructed policy models to identify potential behavioral  consequences . The research proposed in [7], explore d the impact of product recommendations on consumer buying behavior, particularly in the form of reviews and rankings. The paper goes a step further by introducing a recommendation system that focuses on personalized dietary advice. By employing deep learning and genetic algorithms, this system pro vides tailored dietary recommendations based on user experiences with specific products. In [8], authors examine the application of information technology, specifically data mining, data retrieval, and AI in the development of weather and climate predictio n models and present studies with this concept of equality.   The approach outlined in [9] minimize "food loss" by introducing a system suggesting menus and recipes based on available home food items and those nearing expiry. Meanwhile, in [10], the study introduced a model for suggesting dishes based on user prefere nces and consumption history. This involved crafting a classification model to generate a roster of recommended dishes from the user profile. Subsequently, a temporal model utilized dish lists and eating history as inputs to enhance the variety of suggeste d dishes. To enhance accuracy, a set of ingredients was curated to refine the model's performance.   The author s in [11] proposed a new model  to predict weather for implementing a smart IoT based irrigation -based system, this study portrays the potential of using techniques to solve real life problems. The researchers in [12] proposed an automated Irrigation system using the weather prediction to mitigate the misuse of water resources. In the study conducted by authors in [13] propose d a recommendation system which utilizes the Weather patterns using Logistic regression and big data analytics tools. The researchers in  [14] advises how a recommendation -based system advances the business and ease of making complex things easier. The rese archers in their study [15] suggest weather prediction to be a fascinating domain ahead and weather prediction is th e estimation of the future. The study proposed in [16] delves into the building a recommendation system which enhances the user’s interpretability and shopping experience by utilizing the fashion recommendation using Neighbour  Page Rank Algorithm.   The studies suggest that there are multiple challenge s  faced by the food industry as they generate a heavy food wastage due to multiple circumstances. Current studies underscore the potential of machine learning techniques such as Random Forest, kNN, Decision Tree, Naïve Bayes, and Support Vector Machine in a ddressing the weather prediction. However, future research could delve deeper intothe optimization of these algorithms to enhance the predictive accuracy and efficiency. While there is vast research in   weather prediction the integration of it with other food industry remains underdeveloped. The proposed system integrated  weather prediction and food industry for reducing the wastage and improving the business.   III. PROPOSED METHODOLOGY   This section presents a method that  merges advanced machine learning techniques with comprehensive data analysis methods to address the challenges of food wastage reduction and demand prediction in the cloud kitchen sector. The proposed methodology incorporates various steps of weather predi ctions using advanced machine learning algorithms such as Random Forest, Decision Tree, Naïve Bayes, and SHAP. Subsequently, this weather data is meticulously integrated with food industry trends and consumer preferences .    This innovative system leverages the power of current weather data, machine learning, and recommendation engines to deliver personalized meal recommendations based on forecasted weather conditions for Weather user -supplied information, preliminary data pro cessing, product selection, weather forecast modeling, integration and recommendation process mix well, enabling rational decisions to be made based on expected weather conditions on. The generated food recommendations are presented to the user, empowering   them to make informed decisions about their dining choices based on the predicted weather. For prediction of weather 5 different algorithms were implemented to present a showcasing output with reduced errors and a promising result. However, as a future scope integration of additional meteorolo gical data and exploring ensemble  methods to enhance prediction can be done. Also adding up the system’s future prospect can be on elevating the accuracy and user experience. Also adding up features like to adapt through customer trends and integration of  other customer behavior can enhance the recommendation part. A graphical user interface for communication and utilization of these changes can enhance the end user’s experience. In conclusion, while the present study marks a significant step toward addressing food wastage challenges in the cloud kitchen industry, continuous research and development remain essential  to refine and optimize the system's accuracy, responsiveness, and applicability in real -world scenarios.
