Proceeding Paper Optimization of Forecasting Performance in the Retail Sector Using Artificial Intelligence† Hoda Jatte1,* , Sara Belattar2and El Khatir Haimoudi1 1ISISA Team, Department of Computer Science, Faculty of Sciences, Abdelmalek Essaâdi University, Tetouan 93000, Morocco; helkhatir@uae.ac.ma 2Artificial Intelligence Research and Applications Laboratory (AIRA), National School of Business and Management (ENCG), Hassan First University, Settat 26000, Morocco; belattar.sara@uhp.ac.ma *Correspondence: hodajatte30@gmail.com †Presented at the 7th edition of the International Conference on Advanced Technologies for Humanity (ICATH 2025), Kenitra, Morocco, 9–11 July 2025. Abstract In the retail industry, demand forecasting is absolutely crucial for guaranteeing efficient in- ventory and supply chain control. Different artificial intelligence (AI) techniques have been used lately to improve forecasting performance. Demand fluctuation, seasonal patterns, and outside influences continue to create difficulties, though. Using several machine-learning techniques Linear Regression, XGBoost, Random Forest , Decision Tree, Prophet, and LSTM this paper offers a comparative study to forecast product demand. A retail dataset obtained from Kaggle served as the basis for training and testing the forecasting models. The exper- imental results demonstrate that the LSTM model outperforms all others with accuracy, precision, recall, and F1-score of 92.31%, 92.31%, 100.00%, and 96.00%, respectively, followed by Prophet with 85.71%, 92.31%, 92.31%, and 92.31%, respectively, Decision Tree with 93.05%, 75.76%, 76.13%, and 75.94%, respectively, Random Forest with 91.99%, 66.86%, 88.08%, and 76.02%, respectively, XGBoost with 83.21%, 45.70%, 87.84%, and 60.12%, respectively, and Linear Regression with 60.67%, 25.46%, 89.75%, and 39.67%, respectively. These results ver- ify that ensemble and deep learning models can greatly help retailers in raising operational efficiency and notably improve forecasting accuracy. Keywords: retail forecasting; machine learning; deep learning; demand prediction; LSTM; prophet; random forest; XGBoost 1. Introduction In recent years, the retail sector has seen substantial changes as a result of the integra- tion of modern technology, particularly artificial intelligence (AI) [ 1]. Demand forecasting, a vital component of inventory and supply chain management, is increasingly relying on advanced predictive models. Traditional statistical methods frequently fail when dealing with complex, nonlinear patterns in sales data. As a result, machine learning and deep learning approaches have developed as effective methods for enhancing forecast accuracy. This study investigates and compares the performance of various AI algorithms for anticipating retail demand, including Linear Regression, XGBoost, Random Forest, Decision Tree, Prophet, and Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) networks. Using a publicly available dataset from Kaggle [ 2], we present empirical evidence on the strengths and limits of each technique, providing significant insights for shops looking to enhance operations. Eng. Proc. 2025 ,112, 37 https://doi.org/10.3390/engproc2025112037
--- PAGE 2 ---
Eng. Proc. 2025 ,112, 37 2 of 9 Machine learning (ML) [ 3] and artificial intelligence (AI) technologies have emerged as viable tools for improving demand forecasting in recent years. These methods provide excellent predictive accuracy, pattern recognition capabilities, flexibility, and the ability to learn from previous data sets. This research focuses on intelligent forecasting systems that aim to increase operational efficiency and support decision-making in retail envi- ronments. The literature investigates many demand forecasting methods, ranging from simple, interpretable models like Decision Tree and Linear Regression to complex ensemble methods such as Random Forest and XGBoost. Recent models, such LSTM and Prophet, efficiently capture temporal interdependence. This study compares the performance of six models with a Kaggle retail dataset, utilizing metrics such as accuracy, precision, recall, and F1-score. A novel contribution is the systematic comparison of several models using a unified experimental setup that includes both regression and binary classification viewpoints. Furthermore, computational efficiency training and inference time, as well as memory usage is examined, addressing practical factors that are sometimes disregarded in retail demand forecasting. This paper is organized as follows: Section 2 reviews the relevant literature on AI- based forecasting techniques. Section 3 describes the research methodology, including data preprocessing and model implementation. Section 4 presents and discusses experimental results. Section 5 concludes the study and outlines future research directions. 2. Related Literature Recent research has demonstrated the efficacy of Machine Learning and Deep Leraning in retail forecasting. Thivakaran and Ramesh [ 4] discovered that Random Forest outper- formed XGBoost in predicting BigMart sales. Falatouri et al. [ 5] demonstrated that LSTM outperforms SARIMA for some fresh items. Rahman Mahin et al. [ 6] developed a voting regression that outperformed RF and KNN in accuracy. Riachy et al. [ 7] utilized COVID-19 constraints to improve Deep Learning forecast robustness. Darshan et al. [ 8] used pattern mining and Prophet to optimize inventory planning. Ref. [ 9] utilized EDA and ML to demonstrate that Decision Trees accurately forecasted financial earnings. Seyedan et al. [ 10] used LSTM, CNN, and Bayesian averaging to enhance inventory forecasting. Asma ul Husna. [11] used forecasting and fuzzy optimization to make supplier decisions. Tables 1 and 2 provide an overview of recent studies, highlighting their forecasting methods, key contributions, and identified limitations. Table 1. Materials and Methods 3.1. Proposed Methodology This research attempts to provide a dependable and effective forecasting system for retail goods demand. Accurate projections aid in inventory optimization, stockout preven- tion, and supply chain decision-making. Six machine learning and deep learning models were examined using a structured pipeline in Figure 1 to determine the optimal technique. • Data preparation : involves cleaning and preparing the input features. • Feature engineering : to extract relevant temporal and product-related characteristics. • Model training and evaluation : models are trained and evaluated using consistent criteria across all methods. • Performance comparison : using standard classification and regression metrics. • Final model selection : based on experimental results. To ensure a comprehensive evaluation, the following models were chosen: • Linear Regression: a conventional regression method utilized as a reference point. • Decision Tree: a rule-based model known for interpretability. • Random Forest: an ensemble of decision trees that improves generalization. • XGBoost: a gradient boosting model known for accuracy and performance. • Prophet: a time-series forecasting model designed to capture seasonality and trend. All models were developed utilizing an identical dataset and input factors, which encompassed past demand figures, parts of the date (like month, day, and day of the week), and product identifiers. To properly learn temporal patterns, the LSTM model requires reorganizing the input into a sequential shape. To convert the regression task into a binary classification problem, a fixed classification threshold was utilized, defined as the target variable’s mean (Order_Demand = 5248.1). 3.2. Dataset Description The dataset used in this study was obtained from Kaggle and contains historical retail sales records. The primary target variable is Order_Demand, which represents the number of product units requested. The dataset includes the following: • Over 100,000 records; • Key variables: product code, warehouse ID, date, and order demand; • CSV format, with missing and inconsistent values.
--- PAGE 4 ---
Eng. Proc. 2025 ,112, 37 4 of 9 Figure 1. Overview of the proposed forecasting pipeline. 3.3. Data Preprocessing and Binarization The data preprocessing stage involved the following: • Removing null or inconsistent entries; • Transforming categorical variables through label encoding or one-hot encoding; • Extracting time components such as day, month, and weekday from the date; • Exploratory Data Analysis (EDA):The data reveals a clear rising trend, with a 30 day rolling average of 3,069,188 units and weekly seasonality averaging 2,733,319 units. Outliers were deleted to better model training. To convert the regression issue into a binary classification challenge, a specific limit was established using the average value of Order_Demand (5248.1). According to this limit: • Low Demand: instances where Order_Demand ≤5248.1 • High Demand: instances where Order_Demand > 5248.1 This binary transformation mirrors practical business concerns, where choices are frequently based on whether demand reaches a key threshold rather than exact quantities. It also facilitates model evaluation by providing simple measures such as accuracy and recall, which are easier to grasp for operational decision-making and allowed us to evaluate classification performance in addition to regression metrics.
--- PAGE 5 ---
Eng. Proc. 2025 ,112, 37 5 of 9 3.4. Model Implementation Each forecasting model was implemented using Python 3.12 and trained on the same dataset to ensure a fair and consistent comparison. All models were provided with an identical set of input features, which included historical demand values, date based attributes (such as day, month, and day of the week), and product identifiers. Model-specific preprocessing and tuning: LSTM inputs were organized to capture temporal patterns and long-term relationships needed for time series forecasting. To ensure consistency, all models employed the same relevant input features, such as time-based signals and product identification. Feature uniformity and hyperparameter tuning: To ensure consistency, all models used the same input features, regardless of their internal architecture or learning mechanisms. Demand forecasting features such as time-based signals and product identifiers were routinely used. Grid search was used to tune hyper- parameters such as tree depth, learning rate, and regularization in tunable models (e.g., XGBoost, Random Forest). This exhaustive search method systematically evaluated different parameter combina- tions to identify configurations that achieved the best performance on validation data. 3.5. Experimental Setup The experiments were carried out utilizing Google Colab (Python 3.12) and Jupyter Notebook 7.5.0, which allowed for rapid development, code execution, and interactive visualization. These platforms also allowed seamless integration with machine learning libraries and cloud-based resources. To manage and monitor training, MLflow was used to track experiments, log perfor- mance metrics, and store models, assuring repeatability and ease of comparison between runs. Models were built utilizing existing libraries. • Scikit-learn for preprocessing, evaluation metrics, and baseline models; • XGBoost for gradient boosting; • Prophet for additive time series forecasting; • Keras/TensorFlow for implementing the LSTM model. Common Training Settings: • Number of epochs (LSTM): 50 • Batch size: 32 • Train/test split: 80% 20% Additional performance study was undertaken to analyze training and inference times, as well as the models’ memory consumption: - XGBoost • training time: 4.12 s • Training memory use: 6.71 MB • Prediction time: 0.09 s • Peak memory usage during prediction: 1.68 MB. - LSTM • training time: 20.60 s • Maximum memory use during training: 4.68 MB • Prediction time: 0.39 s Evaluation metrics: Accuracy, Precision, Recall, F1-score, R ²(coefficient of determina- tion), RMSE (Root Mean Squared Error). These unified settings ensured that all models were evaluated under consistent conditions, enabling a fair and reliable comparison of their performance.

To assess classification performance, the continuous target variable Order_Demand was binarized using a fixed threshold equal to its mean (5248.1), distinguishing between two classes: Low Demand and High Demand . Discussion The overview show that LSTM and Prophet outperform traditional regression models. LSTM excels at modeling temporal dependencies, whereas Prophet produces accurate and interpretable forecasts with high F1 and recall scores. Among classical models, Decision Tree [ 12] and Random Forest [ 13] perform well, with Random Forest having the lowest RMSE. XGBoost produces balanced measures with reduced precision, while Linear Regression performs poorly. These findings emphasize the relevance of time-aware models in demand forecasting, with LSTM being the preferable method for dynamic inventory management.
--- PAGE 8 ---
Eng. Proc. 2025 ,112, 37 8 of 9 6. Conclusions This study sought to improve demand forecasting in the retail sector by utilizing a variety of artificial intelligence (AI) technologies. Using historical sales data, we conducted a thorough comparison of the performance of various forecasting models, including Linear Regression, Random Forest, XGBoost, and LSTM. The findings showed that sophisticated machine learning algorithms, particularly XGBoost and LSTM, outperformed traditional methods for predicting future demand. XGBoost displayed great stability and efficiency in processing structured data, whereas LSTM excelled at identifying temporal dependencies, making it ideal for time-series forecasting. These findings highlight the significant potential of AI-based models to improve demand prediction accuracy and, as a result, optimize inventory management and supply chain operations in the retail business. By synchronizing supply and demand, AI-driven forecasting enables retailers to make data-driven decisions, save money, and enhance customer happiness. Automating demand forecasts eliminates stockouts and overstocking while optimizing resources and reducing waste. Future work could include conducting more exploratory data analysis to find trends and better feature engineering, as well as including external elements such as weather and marketing. Hybrid models that combine sequential and non-sequential methods may improve forecast accuracy and robustness. Demand Forecasting System for Small Business  Optimization    Mary Cherian, Aniket Jane, Deep ak, G Sinchana, MD. Modasscer  Department of Information Science and Engineering  Dayananda Sagar Collage of Engineering,   Bangalore, India.  mary-ise@dayanandasagar.edu, aniketmjane@gmail.com, deepakosame888@gmail.com  sinchanabhandari9@gmail.com, mohammed.modasscer17@gmail.com  Abstract —Demand forecasting is a fundamental element in  efficient inventory control and supply chain management  through the ability of firms to anticipate future demand and  devise methods to service that demand accordingly. The  present paper explores two major approaches to demand  forecasting: the SARIMA model and the Deep AR model.  SARIMA is a more traditional statistical approach and  exploits autoregressive, integrated, and moving average  components along with seasonal adjustments to pick up  trends and seasonality in the sales data. DeepAR is the most  advanced deep learning-based probabilistic approach in  modelling complex time series dynamics and relationships  within interconnected  time series. This paper details the  process of demand forecasting starting from data collection,  preparation, and cleaning stages to model building, testing,  and validation. SARIMA was able to clearly prove its fit for  short- to medium-term forecasting, enabling it to be very  efficient in keeping track of inventory stocks and preventing  stockouts. However, DeepAR does outperform SARIMA  when using large dynamic datasets where non-linear patterns  come into play, providing probabilistic forecasts that quantify  uncertainty. Our findings have illuminated the strengths and  weaknesses of both methods. Given stable, well-structured  datasets, SARIMA is a robust choice. However, DeepAR's  advanced capabilities are well-suited for businesses dealing  with complex and dynamic conditions. Firms can optimize  operations to adapt to market fluctuations and even make  data-driven decisions to enhance efficiency and customer  satisfaction by using these tools.  Keywords—Demand forecasting, SARIMA, DeepAR, time  series analysis, inventory management, probabilistic forecasting.  I. INTRODUCTION  Demand forecasting is critical to modern business practice  in order to maintain a competitive edge: it works together with  efficient management of inventories and scheduling of  production, and it controls supply chain activities. Being  familiar with the meaning of demand forecasting is very  crucial since it involves the intervals for which the demands  for the particular goods or services are expected. Good  forecasting prevents any costs associated with overstocking  and understocking, improves customer service levels, and  improves the efficiency of the supply chain. The demand  forecasting enables businesses to make informed decisions  that align resources with market needs.  II. FORECASTING MODELS AND MATHEMATICAL  REPRESENTATION   Forecasting methods in time series analysis have been  developed with the aim of improving and estimating the range of predictions. Most of these methods are mainly used in the  modelling of time-dependent processes thanks to the  incorporation of structure: trends, seasonality, and other  regimes present in the time series data.  A. SARIMA Model  1) SARIMA Model:   The Seasonal Auto Regressive Integrated Moving Average  (SARIMA) model is a well-known time series forecasting  method that handles both trend and seasonality in the data. It  is mathematically expressed as:  SARIMA (p, d, q) (P, D, Q) s      Where:   x p, d, q (non-seasonal order):   x p: Number of lag observations used to predict the  current value. Example: If p = 2, the model uses the  past 2 values.  x d: Number of times the data is differenced to make it  stationary. Example: If d = 1, the model uses the  change between consecutive values.  x q: Number of past forecast errors used to predict the  current value. Example: If q = 1, the model uses the  previous error term.  x P, D, Q (seasonal order):  x s: Length of the seasonality cycle. Example: For  monthly data with yearly seasonality → s = 12, For  weekly data with yearly seasonality → s = 52  x P: Similar to p, but for the seasonal component, uses  past seasonal values.  x D: Number of times the data is differenced at the  seasonal level to remove seasonal trend.  Example: If D = 1 and s = 12 (monthly data with  yearly seasonality), the model uses the difference  between this month and the same month last year.  x Q: Similar to q, but for seasonal errors.  SARIMA works very well for industries such as retail,  manufacturing, and logistics that have a demand curve with a  certain level of oscillation because of the prevailing  seasonality, for short- to medium-range forecasting periods.  However, these types of models often have a challenge in  capturing the irregular swings and structural changes when  there is an intricate structure contained in the database used  even within dynamic markets. 2025 5th International Conference on Intelligent Technologies (CONIT)  Karnataka, India. June 20-22, 2025 979-8-3315-2233-9/25/$31.00 ©2025 IEEE 12025 5th International Conference on Intelligent Technologies (CONIT) | 979-8-3315-2233-9/25/$31.00 ©2025 IEEE | DOI: 10.1109/CONIT65521.2025.11167430 Authorized licensed use limited to: Mukesh Patel School of Technology & Engineering. Downloaded on July 08,2026 at 18:54:05 UTC from IEEE Xplore.  Restrictions apply. 
--- PAGE 2 ---
 2) DeepAR Model    Yt+1=f (Yt, Yt −1,..., Y1)   Where:  x Yt+1 is the forecasted value at the next step.  x  Yt, Yt−1., Y1 represent the sequence of past  observed values, and the function f is a non-linear function learned by a recurrent neural network (RNN).  B. Combined Model:    y^t combined=(y^t SARIMA+y^t DeepAR)/2    Where:   x y^t combined: Combined forecast at time t; the  average of SARIMA and DeepAR predictions.  x y^t SARIMA: Forecast at time t from the SARIMA  model, which captures linear trends and seasonality.  x y^t DeepAR: Forecast at time t from the DeepAR  model, which learns non-linear and complex patterns. Simple averaging technique combines the outputs of both models equally.  The SARIMA model (1) works well for seasonal and  linear patterns, while DeepAR (2) handles complex and changing data using deep learning. To get the best of both, we take the average of their predictions to create a combined forecast (3). This helps improve overall accuracy and makes  the model more reliable.  The integration of traditional statistical methods such as  SARIMA (1) with advanced deep learning models like DeepAR (2) leads to a hybrid forecasting framework that captures both seasonality and nonlinearity. The final ensemble forecast (3) benefits from the interpretability of SARIMA and the adaptability of DeepAR, offering improved accuracy and resilience for demand prediction in uncertain or highly variable environments.  III. LITERATURE  SURVEY  [1] Tereza Šustrová et. al. from the Brno University of  Technology, explored the challenges posed by the conventional inventory models. With this, they recommended the application of artificial neural networks (ANNs) due to the  complexities posed by supply chains. The authors  experimented with different configurations of ANNs, while the training function TRAINGDX and transfer function TANSIG were employed. This research applied data analysis and pre-processing with MS Office Excel and the model creation; testing was done with the Neural Network Toolbox of MATLAB software. In this study, the optimal model that had eight hidden neurons was very effective (R² = 0.99) and had very low Mean Squared Error (MSE = 0.029) and found that Managing Neural Networks can help even to determine how much order to place in order to enhance management of order cycle dealing with inventory levels problems.  [2] Maryam Zohdi et al.  of Sharif University of  Technology and Hamad bin of Khalifa University have put  forward the idea of advancing demand prediction by machine  learning algorithms. The authors discussed the limitations of oft-used statistical approaches such as ARIMA when applied to complex non-linear data. Following this, they, perhaps  excitedly, started looking up some of the more advanced  approaches like Extreme Learning Machine (ELM), Decision  Trees, Gradient Boosting, Multi-Layer Perceptron (MLP), K-nearest Neighbors (KNN), and so on. The models used acquired data from a marketing campaign referred to as Black Friday, with some customer inclination and situation related assessment criteria, Mean Squared Error (MSE) and Mean Adjusted Error (MAE), etc. The outcomes showed that ELM and MLP were in all likelihood better than traditional approaches meaning that they co uld be depended upon as far  as maps of their structure were concerned.   [3] José Eduardo de Carvalho Lima et al. made a  systematic review that provides some areas for improvement regarding demand forecasting and planning of production  systems, but it is not very useful practically since it does  provide a comparative assessment of different models. On the  background of the study, some models are very complex and therefore, applicability can be a challenge. In this regard, the scope of research is imbalanced as some crucial areas remain inadequately addressed. In any case, the paper is helpful in identifying weaknesses of the existing research, but it could have improved its contributions through more comprehensive treatment of some particular techniques used.  [4] Aditya Chawla et al. analysed applications of ANNs in  demand forecasting. Scenarios presented the capability of ANNs in demand forecasting. Results thereby attained reflected satisfying for using ANNs in forecasting, mainly in the integration of complex data patterns. The huge computational costs involved with models developed using ANNs will be one major drawback to this kind of model. The  computational complexity does cause a particular problem  when very large data sets are involved, since tools like MATLAB, unfortunately, tend to limit the ability to scale up models that would accommodate large volumes of data satisfactorily.  [5] Muhammad Adnan Khan et al. at Lahore Garrison  University designed and implemented a business intelligence driven demand forecasting system which integrates machine learning into its workings. Once they applied the deepAR algorithm on AWS Sage maker, they could analyse sales and inventory data with up to 92.38% accuracy. Most importantly, this model can be of substantial assistance to companies those want to reduce wasteful production and improve the utilization of available resources, especially those in need of  new products but have little data to consult.  [6] Real Carbonneau et al., of Concordia University,  studied some machine learning techniques, particularly NN, RNN, and SVM, in addressing bullwhip effect in supply chain demand forecasting. These advanced techniques were then compared with traditional methods in the form of moving averages and regression models with both simulated data and real data. Though RNNs and SVMs have shown an improvement in the accuracy of their forecasting, the differences weren't statistically significant. SVMs provided a solid mathematical base to excel in dealing with complex time series with minimum overfitting. Therefore, the use of machine learning is expected to increase the quality of forecasts in order to streamline the supply chain.  [7] Fattah et al. investigated demand forecasting using the  ARIMA model in a Moroccan food company, employing the  Box-Jenkins methodology with historical data from 2010– 2 Authorized licensed use limited to: Mukesh Patel School of Technology & Engineering. Downloaded on July 08,2026 at 18:54:05 UTC from IEEE Xplore.  Restrictions apply. 
--- PAGE 3 ---
 2015. They developed and evaluated several ARIMA models  based on criteria like AIC, SBC, and maximum likelihood,  selecting ARIMA (1, 0, 1) as the most suitable. The chosen  model demonstrated high accuracy in predicting demand, facilitating improved production planning, inventory management, and cost reduction. This study highlighted ARIMA’s effectiveness in forecasting demand for perishable goods, ensuring better decision-making and enhanced supply chain efficiency.  [8] Naoufel Cheikhrouhou et al. performed a review on  collaborative forecasting that combines mathematical models with event-based fuzzy judgments. The study highlights the problems of using these methods because of different perceptions by the forecasters and the difficulty of integrating contextual knowledge. Although it emphasizes collaboration  within firms, it pays less attention to inter-firm processes, thus  creating a gap in the overall analysis. The research explains  the limitations of conventional forecasting models and proposes systematized solutions to improve predictions. However, it does not offer a critical comparison of various techniques available and, therefore is less useful practically. Nevertheless, the study outlines important insights related to the combining of human judgement and models with demand forecasting within changing environments.  [9] Fatima Zohra Benhamida et al. published a review  paper on demand forecasting methods for time-series data. The paper was proposing a novel demand forecasting tool for inventory control. In such a study, the strength in integrating statistical and machine learning methods is presented. However, such a study is lacking in practical comparative insights into its application across diversified industries.  Given the background of the study, the complexity in hybrid  methods gives rise to problems in adoption. Additionally, the scope is somewhat imbalanced because it focuses less on the area of intermittent demand forecasting and new product introduction forecasting. The paper identifies substantial gaps and is very helpful for developing robust tools for forecasting.  [10] Kanik et al. investigated the application of regression- based machine learning techniques for forecasting sales within supply chain operations. The study focused on leveraging predictive analytics to analyse sales data, identifying demand trends and patterns. Their findings highlighted how regression models could support supply chain optimization and enhance inventory management decisions.  [11] Sukolkit et al.proposed an innovative approach to  inventory management that integrates demand forecasting techniques specifically tailored for the steel industry. The study emphasized the importance of open innovation in addressing inventory challenges, utilizing advanced forecasting methods to predict demand trends accurately. Their research provided insights into optimizing inventory management processes in a dynamic and complex industrial environment.  [12] Khan et al. (2020) introduced a demand forecasting  model enhanced by business intelligence and machine learning techniques. The study highlighted the integration of machine learning algorithms to improve the accuracy and efficiency of demand predictions. By leveraging business intelligence tools, the model provided actionable insights,  supporting strategic decision-making processes across various  business operations. IV. PROPOSED  SYSTEM  A. Data Source      Fig. 1.  Overview of System Data Source  Figure 1 depicts the framework that links an inventory  management system to a web interface. Product and logistics data are processed using SARIMA, DeepAR and the combined model to generate individual forecasts.   The proposed system relies on two key data sources: sales  data and inventory data, both of which play a vital role in generating accurate predictions for unit sales. Each dataset is categorized using unique Stock Keeping Unit identities (SKU IDs) and Store identities, ensuring that the forecasts are highly specific to individual products and their respective store  locations. Historical sales data captures trends in customer  demand, while inventory data ensures that predictions are aligned with stock availability, helping to avoid overstocking or stock shortages and help in the process of inventory management.   This data is uploaded into a web interface where it  undergoes preprocessing, such as cleaning and formatting, to prepare it for machine learning analysis. Advanced algorithms are then applied to identify patterns and correlations between various factors influencing demand. By combining real-time and historical data labelled with SKU IDs and Store IDs, the system produces reliable, product-specific, and location-based forecasts. The system can also incorporate external factors, such as seasonal trends or market shifts, to enhance prediction accuracy further. This comprehensive approach enables  businesses to make informed decisions, streamline inventory  management, and improve overall operational efficiency, ultimately boosting customer satisfaction.  B. Machine Learning Models  1) SARIMA AND DEEP AR:  The SARIMA model is a statistical method of time series  forecasting aimed at data modelling that follows the seasonal  patterns. Its methodology is essentially an extension of the  ARIMA model, taking the seasonal into account, and is particularly well-suited for datasets in which seasonality makes a significant difference to demand. The three components on which SARIMA is based are Autoregressive, Integrated, and Moving Average, with their seasonal counterparts. Notation SARIMA (p, d, q) (P, D, Q, m) where  p, d, and q depict the non-seasonal AR, differencing and MA  terms. Their seasonal equivalents, P, D, Q, and m with m as  3 Authorized licensed use limited to: Mukesh Patel School of Technology & Engineering. Downloaded on July 08,2026 at 18:54:05 UTC from IEEE Xplore.  Restrictions apply. 
--- PAGE 4 ---
 the number of observations in each season are denoted, The  SARIMA method focuses on an exhaustive analysis of the  data to work out the underlying trends, seasonality, and noise.  This involves exploratory data analysis, where visualizations and various statistical tests, such as the autocorrelation and partial autocorrelation functions, help identify possible seasonal lags and check for stationarity. Stationarity is a crucial assumption in SARIMA modelling, since it ensures that the statistical properties of the time series, mean and variance, are invariant with respect to time. In the case of non-stationary in data, differencing is done. Seasonal differencing is specially used to remove periodic fluctuations, while regular differencing removes overall trends.  This paper will focus on the usage of a robust methodology  for forecasting demand through SARIMA and DeepAR  models and a stronger methodology for accurate and more  interpretable results. The dataset has historical sales data  supplemented with economic indicators and seasonal trends to capture demand fluctuations comprehensively. Data was sourced from internal company repositories and external publicly available databases. To ensure sufficient coverage of patterns and anomalies, a daily granularity spanning five years is chosen, offering both short-term and long-term perspectives critical for effective forecasting.  Before model application, rigorous data pre-processing  steps are undertaken. Missing values, a common challenge in time series datasets, are handled using linear interpolation, preserving the temporal continuity essential for accurate forecasting. Outliers could skew model predictions. They are detected using interquartile ranges and replaced with median values, hence upholding the integrity of the dataset. The  variance of the data is stabilized through log scaling, and  differencing is used to ensure stationarity; this is critical for models as complicated as SARIMA. These pre-processing steps ensure that the dataset is optimized for reliable analysis  and modelling.  For model choice, both SARIMA and DeepAR are chosen  for their complementary strengths in handling seasonal and non-linear time series data. SARIMA is most suited for     Fig. 2.  System Architecture datasets exhibiting clear seasonal patterns, as it can include  autoregressive, moving average, and seasonal components.  This makes it strong in modelling periodic demand variations.  However, DeepAR, a probabilistic forecasting model that uses recurrent neural networks (RNN), proves superior in grasping complex dependencies present in the data. The ability of deep learning models to deal with covariates, and hence outputting probabilities for the underlying predictions, is extremely helpful in dynamic patterning of missing data.  As shown in figure 2, it depicts a forecasting pipeline with  data preprocessing, feature engineering, and two model SARIMA (statistical) and DeepAR (deep learning). Their outputs are fused using an ensemble approach to generate the final forecast.  The train is split into 70% for training, 20% for validation,  and 10% for testing so as to allow robust model evaluation and  generalization. SARIMA's parameters, comprising  autoregressive (p), differencing (d), and moving average (q) components, together with seasonal counterparts (P, D, Q, m) are selected with grid search and the model is assessed with Akaike Information Criterion (AIC). In DeepAR, hyper parameters like the number of RNN layers, hidden units, and learning rate are tuned with Bayesian search to ensure the model is both efficient and accurate. Model performance is evaluated using several metrics, namely Mean Absolute Error, Mean Squared Error, and Mean Absolute Percentage Error. Mean Absolute Error gives a simple average measure of deviation, while the Mean Squared Error punishes larger errors, making it more sensitive to significant deviations in forecasts.   Mean absolute percentage error is particularly useful in  providing an intuitive interpretation of the accuracy of  forecasts relative to observations. These metrics collectively ensure a balanced assessment of model efficacy across different dimensions of error sensitivity and interpretability. The final choice of model is guided by comparisons on the validation dataset. Both SARIMA and DeepAR are compared to the evaluation metrics defined. SARIMA's interpretability together with its statistical basis is compared with the ability of DeepAR to model non-linear dependencies as well as the capability of integrating covariates. In situations where both have similar performance, operational factors, such as computational efficiency and scalability, play a decisive role. The model with the minimum average Mean absolute percentage errorover validation  datasets is preferred for  deployment with consideration of practical implementation  aspects, aligning with business objectives. With this  methodology, then, the SARIMA and DeepAR are combined in order to provide robust, accurate, and interpretive demand forecasts. Thus, by incorporating rigorous data pre-processing, systematic parameter tuning, and comprehensive evaluation, this approach will ensure that  the model chosen is not only  technically sound but also operationally viable. It will allow businesses to make informed decisions and optimize inventory levels and, hence, improve general supply chain efficiency.    Moreover, businesses must ensure that their forecasting  models are adaptable to market changes and external disruptions. Regular updates to the models using the latest data can significantly improve their accuracy. Additionally,  integrating domain knowledge with machine learning  techniques can enhance model interpretability and decision-making. Future research can explore hybrid approaches  4 Authorized licensed use limited to: Mukesh Patel School of Technology & Engineering. Downloaded on July 08,2026 at 18:54:05 UTC from IEEE Xplore.  Restrictions apply. 
--- PAGE 5 ---
 incorporating real-time external factors such as economic  indicators and competitor strategies. This would further  enhance forecasting accuracy and operational efficiency,  ultimately leading to better business outcomes.  V. SIMULATION  RESULTS  This simulation demonstrates demand forecasting using  two models, SARIMA and DeepAR, to predict product sales for a specific SKU (216419) at a particular store (Store ID: 8091). The interface allows users to upload data, set a prediction horizon (default of 12 weeks), and input store and SKU details. The SARIMA model, a traditional statistical approach, provides predictions with an accuracy of 50.76%, reflecting its limitation in capturing complex temporal patterns and irregularities. In contrast, the DeepAR model, which is based on deep learning, achieves a much higher accuracy of 81.0%, indicating its ability to model time-series  data and identify trends a superior manne  To further enhance reliability, a combined model is created  by merging predictions from both SARIMA and DeepAR, resulting in an overall accuracy of 77.14%. This is a hybrid approach that balances the strengths of both models, thereby  offering a robust forecasting solution. All three models  predicted values are presented with accuracy themetrics to allow comparisons. A graph visualizes the combined forecast over the prediction period, illustrating sales trends and fluctuations for better decision-making.By integrating visualization tools, the interface provides actionable insights, enabling businesses to respond proactively to demand fluctuations. The ability to fine-tune parameters for both models ensure flexibility and adaptability to diverse  forecasting scenarios, enhancing decision-making and  operational efficiency. This simulation, therefore shows how effective advanced deep learning approaches, such as DeepAR, are in demand forecasting and the importance of amalgamation of traditional and modern approach towards achieving improved accuracy metrics to allow comparison.    Fig. 3.  Web Interface    As shown in figure 3, the interface allows users to upload a  CSV file, specify the number of weeks for prediction, and  enter store and SKU IDs before generating a forecast.      Fig. 4.  Predicted Sales Comparison Across Models  The table in figure 4, compares predicted unit sales from  SARIMA, DeepAR, and their combined model for a specific SKU and store. Accuracy improves from SARIMA (50.76%)  to DeepAR (81.0%), with the combined model achieving  77.14% accuracy.    Fig. 5.  Prediction Graph Interface  As shown in figure 5, the graph visualizes the final  forecasted unit sales over time for a specific SKU and store,  based on an ensemble of SARIMA and DeepAR model.  The ability to fine-tune parameters for both models  ensures flexibility and adaptability to diverse forecasting scenarios, enhancing decision-making and operational efficiency. This simulation, therefore shows how effective  advanced deep learning approaches, such as DeepAR, are in  demand forecasting and the importance of amalgamation of traditional and modern approach towards achieving improved accuracy metrics to allow comparison. Future research can explore hybrid approaches incorporating real-time external factors such as economic indicato rs and competitor strategies.  This would further enhance forecasting accuracy and operational efficiency, ultimately leading to better business outcomes.  VI. CONCLUSION  This research explores the approach of creating a demand  forecasting system using the strengths of two powerful models: SARIMA and DeepAR, for predicting future sales in the retail environment. The study analysed SARIMA and DeepAR  models for demand forecasting in small businesses. SARIMA  performed well for structured, seasonal data but struggled with complex patterns, whereas DeepAR, a deep learning approach,  5 Authorized licensed use limited to: Mukesh Patel School of Technology & Engineering. Downloaded on July 08,2026 at 18:54:05 UTC from IEEE Xplore.  Restrictions apply. 
--- PAGE 6 ---
 achieved higher accuracy (81.0% vs. SARIMA’s 50.76%). A  hybrid model combining both improved accuracy to 77.14%,  demonstrating the benefits of integrating statistical and  machine learning techniques.  The demonstration showed how such systems can deal with  seasonal trends and even empirical multivariate data, resulting in accurate and reliable forecasts of retail demand. The evaluation of the hybrid forecasting model indicates improved prediction accuracy over standalone models for small business inventory forecasting. The other key advantages of the system are scalability and efficiency. It could process quite large volumes of highly needed data within reasonable time limits, thus being ideal for high-volume retail environments. The user-friendly system allows singular stakeholders to load data and understand what was forecasted using clarity in  visualizations and accuracy metrics. The system thus stands  as an efficient tool for inventory and sales planning where  retailers can optimize stock levels. This data-driven forecasting tool can help small businesses minimize waste, streamline operations, and make more informed decisions for sustainable growth.   VII. FUTURE  WORK  While the proposed demand forecasting system performs  effectively, there are several avenues for improvement and expansion. One of the primary areas for enhancement is the incorporation of external variables, such as promotions, holidays, weather conditions, and economic indicators. These factors can significantly influence demand patterns, and integrating them into the forecasting models could further improve the accuracy and robustness of predictions. This will  result in more precise and context-aware forecasts, making it  better suited for dynamic and unpredictable retail environments. Another direction for future work is to refine the model to handle larger datasets more efficiently. Additionally, integrating real-time data processing would allow continuous updating of predictions based on current sales trends, improving short-term forecasting accuracy and enabling quicker decision-making for retailers. Combining SARIMA and DeepAR with XGBoost or LSTM (Long Short-Term Memory), could improve the system’s ability to handle irregular sales patterns or highly volatile demand. Furthermore, providing users with the ability to customize model settings and select relevant external variables would increase the flexibility of the system, making it adaptable to a wider range of retail scenarios and business needs.  Future work can also explore external factors like  economic trends. Advanced forecasting techniques will empower small businesses with data-driven insights for  efficiency and growth.     1 AI-Driven Predictive Analytics for Sustainable  Restaurant Operations and Waste Minimization    Padmavathy .M1, Dharmendra H2, K. Thilagavathi3, Burri Naresh4, P. Praba Devi5, Mohit Tiwari6    1Department of Commerce, SRM Institute of Science & Technology, Vadapalani City Campus, Chennai, Tamil Nadu, India   2Department of Commerce, CHRIST (Deemed to be University), Bangalore, India   3Department of Commerce, Saveetha school of law, SIMATS, Saveetha University, Chennai, Tamil Nadu, India  4Department of CSE(CS), CVR College of Engineering, Hyderabad, Telangana  5Department of Management Studies, Sona College of Technology, Salem, Tamil Nadu, India  6Department of Computer Science and Engineering, Bhara ti Vidyapeeth’s College of Engineering, Paschim Vihar, Delhi, India     Email id: padmavam@srmist.edu.in, dharmendrh1000@gmail.com, thilagaramesh1995@gmail.com, naresh.burri@gmail.com,  prabadevi@sonabusinessschool.com, mohit.t.bvcoe@gmail.com     Abstract- There is mounting pressure in the restaurant  business to minimize the waste of their operations and use  of resources, and still be able to make a profit.  Unreasonable forecasting, over-procurement, and poor  management of resources are the key causes of  environmental and financial waste. As a potential solution  to the issue, it presents an AI-based Predictive Analytics  Framework (AID-PAF), which combines both a  Temporal-Fusion Neural Architecture (TFNA), which is  an asset demand prediction framework, and a waste- conscious linear programming model used to solve an  inventory and resources allocation problem. Real  restaurant operational datasets were used to test the  system in a hybrid AnyLogic-MATLAB simulator.  Experimental findings show that the proposed framework  achieved 40%, 18%, 15%, and 21% reductions in the food  waste, energy use, water use, and costs, respectively, and  in addition enhanced the accuracy of the forecast, MAPE  of 6.5%, the customer fill-rate 96.2%, and the  Sustainability Score 78.7. The results prove that  predictive analytics based on AI can greatly contribute to  the sustainability, efficiency, and profitability of  restaurant operations by making intelligent decisions with  the assistance of data.    Keywords— AI-Driven Predictive Analytics Framework,  Temporal-Fusion Neural Architecture, Sustainability- Driven Resource Utilization Model, Waste Reduction  Rate.    I. INTRODUCTION     The food service industry is among the most  dynamic and resource-intensiveindustries in the  global economy. Restaurant businesses are known to  use a lot of raw materials, energy, and water, and  produce a huge amount of waste during the  procurement, preparation, and service processes. The  faster urbanization, the changing consumer demand, and the short shelf life of perishable products make  resource management even more complicated [1].  According to recent estimates, it is estimated that  about a third of all food produced is spoiled before  consumption, which leads to substantial emissions of  greenhouse gases and economic losses [2]. The main  sources of this wastage in the restaurant operations  are imprecise demand prediction, poor inventory  control, and inefficient use of resources. Therefore,  it has become necessary to create smart systems that  can forecast consumption patterns and manage the  flow of resources in order to achieve sustainable  development [3]. Conventional restaurant  management systems use a lot of manual estimation  and historical averages that cannot be able to  identify the nonlinear trends due to seasonality,  weather variability, and social events [4]. The  current literature has used statistical regression or  rudimentary machine-learning models (decision  trees and support vector machines) to predict food  demand; nevertheless, both models tend to be unable  to extrapolate among heterogeneous data [5]. The  challenges found in literature research comprise  inadequate information, a lack of real-time  flexibility, the non-existence of multi-objective  optimization of cost and sustainability, and little  waste-specific modeling [6]. Furthermore, the vast  majority of the previous literature only touched upon  sales prediction but did not connect the outcomes of  the forecasting to specific procurement or resource- allocation plans. Consequently, there are still cases  of a mismatched supply and demand in restaurants,  which result in overstocking, a lack of the use of  resources in the kitchen, and carbon footprints that  are not necessary [7]. It is inspired by these  difficulties and presents an AI-based Predictive  Analytics Framework (AID-PAF) to reduce wastage 2025 Tenth International Conference on Science Technology Engineering and Mathematics (ICONSTEM) | 979-8-3315-6187-1/25/$31.00 ©2025 IEEE | DOI: 10.1109/ICONSTEM65670.2025.11374322 Authorized licensed use limited to: Mukesh Patel School of Technology & Engineering.       2 and promote sustainability at every level of  restaurant functioning. The framework draws the  strength of temporal models based on deep learning  to predict demand precisely and uses linear  programming to allocate resources optimally [8]. It  is through a combination of predictive analytics and  real-time operational feedback that the system can  predict consumption as well as dynamically modify  procurement and equipment schedules, which  provide a quantifiable sustainability benefit [9]. The  solution is consistent with the global environmental  objectives that would promote a digital  transformation of food systems to be greener and  more efficient.     The main tasks of this work are fourfold: to  formulate a dynamic forecasting model that provides  accurate prediction of item-based demand based on  both the past and current factors; to add waste  sensitivity strategy that reduces excess food and  energy usage; to verify the model with real  restaurant datasets and using digital-twin simulation;  and to show measurable changes in waste reduction,  cost savings and sustainability performance in  general [10]. The proposed AID- PAF framework  delivered good outcomes in the simulation. The  system used a hybrid AnyLogic-MATLAB  environment that had about 50,000 operational  records, and it saved food waste by 40% and energy  usage by 18%, and water by 15%, but was still able  to continue with a high customer fill-rate of 96.2%  [11]. The accuracy of the forecast also rose to a  MAPE of 6.5%, which was almost 20% better than  the conventional models, including LSTM and  XGBoost. Moreover, the procurement cost dropped  by 2%1, and the total Sustainability Score rose to  78.7 as opposed to 56.4, which proves the efficiency  of the predictive modeling-optimization mechanisms  integration. The most important contributions are as  follows:The Development of a Temporal-Fusion  Neural Architecture adapted to restaurant demand  forecasting at a high temporal resolution.  Construction of a waste-conscious linear  programming model to optimize the procurement of  ingredients and the use of resources. Sustainability  metrics integration and food, energy, and water  parameter computation into a combined performance  index. Real-time assessment of operational  performance through implementation of a hybrid  digital-twin simulation in AnyLogic and MATLAB.  The clear expression of a drastic reduction in waste,  energy consumption, and profitability [12]. The rest  of the paper is structured in the following way: Section II will give a summary of related work and  technological background. Section III gives the  proposed methodology with details such as  mathematical models and characteristics of the  dataset. In the fourth section, the experimental setup  and the results that have been acquired due to the  simulation are discussed. Section V concludes and  gives the future research directions for multi- restaurant scalability and real-time adaptation of the  IoT-enabled. III.P ROPOSED WORK    Dynamic Demand Forecasting Using Temporal- Fusion Neural Architecture (TFNA)  The first component of the suggested framework is  concerned with the high-temporal accuracy of the  prediction of the restaurant customer demand and  the use of ingredients. Correct demand forecasting is  an important part of leveling procurement,  preparation, and inventory decision-making to  reduce food waste and enhance operational  efficiency. Intelligent Workflow for Sustainable  Restaurant Prediction and Optimization         Predictive Inventory Optimization through Waste- Aware Linear Programming  The second module, based on the predicted demand  as generated in the forecasting model, takes  advantage of optimizing inventory acquisition  decisions and reducing food waste and stock  shortages. The conflict between restaurant inventory  management involves the following: overordering  will result in spoilage and high holding costs, and  underordering will result in customer dissatisfaction  and loss of revenue. To fulfill this equilibrium, a  waste-conscious linear programming (LP) model is  created. The goal function will be to minimize the  total cost of overstocking and understocking in  equation (4),    𝑚𝑖𝑛 𝑄𝑖= [𝐶𝑜max 0,𝑄𝑖 +𝐶𝑠max⁡(0,𝐷𝑖−𝑄𝑖)]𝑛 𝑖=1     (4)    Where 𝑄i is the ordered quantity of ingredient 𝑖, D i is  the demand predicted by Module A, Cois the  overstock cost per unit due to food spoilage, and Cs  is the shortage cost per unit due to loss in service or  customer dissatisfaction. The model works within  the following constraints in equations (5) & (6),    𝑄𝑖𝑚𝑖𝑛≤ 𝑄𝑖≤ 𝑄𝑖𝑚𝑎𝑥,   ∀𝑖        (5)     𝑐𝑖𝑄𝑖≤𝐵𝑛 𝑖=1          (6)    Where B is the total budget constraint and 𝑐𝑖 is the  price of procuring each ingredient. The optimization  process is solved iteratively with the help of the  updated demand predictions, which allows making  dynamic changes in the procurement schedules for  each day. The strategy will reduce waste by avoiding  excess orders and increase profitability by  distributing inventory cost-efficiently, which directly  helps to sustain the operations of the restaurant. In  Fig.2, this architecture diagram defines the dynamic  and real-time data exchange between sensors of the  restaurant, the AI predictive core, and performance  evaluation modules to guarantee the provision of  dynamic monitoring, the detection of anomalies, and  the sustainability-driven operational decision- making. Server-Centered Architecture of the AI- Driven Predictive Analytics Framework    Sustainability-Driven Resource Utilization Model  (SDRUM)  Although the reduction of food waste is an important  part of sustainable restaurant management, other  environmental factors, including energy and water  use, and packaging waste, are important variables  that contribute to the overall sustainability  performance. In order to optimize these parameters  in a holistic way, a Sustainability-Driven Resource  Utilization Model (SDRUM) is shown as a multi- objective optimization problem. This will guarantee the best allocation of resources,  a smaller carbon footprint, and better sustainability,  without affecting the service quality or production  efficiency. In Fig.3, it is a layered hybrid model that  connects historical and environmental data with the  neural network prediction, sustainability alignment,  and waste optimization processes that allow an  accurate adaptive forecasting and smart use of  resources in the present restaurant systems.      Fig 3. Hybrid Predictive Architecture for Demand  Forecasting and Sustainability Integration    Intelligent Data Fusion and Adaptive Model  Training Pipeline (IDF-ATP)  To achieve predictive capability that is reliable and  context-sensitive, an Intelligent Data Fusion and  Adaptive Model Training Pipeline (IDF-ATP) was  created. This module assembles a strong piece of  information by combining multi-source information  that comprises both transactional and environmental  factors affecting the operation of the restaurants. The  basic dataset was obtained through the Food  Demand Forecasting Dataset, which is present in the  UCI Machine Learning Repository, which has  detailed historical restaurant transactions, menu- level sales, weather conditions, and calendar-based  indicators of seasonality. The secondary data  included waste-specific parameters of the Food  Waste Reduction Alliance (FWRA) survey and  Kaggle Restaurant Waste Prediction dataset, which  adds the sustainability aspect of the model. The  standardized data set consisted of about 50,000  records across 24 restaurants, and 32 organized  features of the type of ingredient, the purchase  volume, temperature, humidity, and the volume of  waste. The preprocessing of the data was done by  Authorized licensed use limited to: Mukesh Patel School of Technology & Engineering. Downloaded on July 08,2026 at 19:10:35 UTC from IEEE Xplore.       6 normalizing it with Min-Max scaling in equation  (10),    𝑥′=(𝑥−𝑥𝑚𝑖𝑛 ) (𝑥𝑚𝑎𝑥−𝑥𝑚𝑖𝑛 )          (10)    To ensure a model is generalized, the dataset was  split into 70% training, 15% validation, and 15%  testing samples. The Python TensorFlow 2.15 /  Keras framework was used for model training with  the help of a GPU accelerator to make the  computations efficient. Fine-tuning of  hyperparameters, learning rate, batch size, and  dropout ratio was done using Bayesian optimization.  The system had converged in 75 epochs, and the  early stopping was done when the validation loss  dropped below 0.05, as it would guarantee the best  generalization without overfitting.    Cyber-Physical Integration and Real-Time Digital  Twin Simulation Framework (CPRT-DSF)  A Cyber-Physical Integration and Real-Time Digital  Twin Simulation Framework (CPRT-DSF) was  introduced to guarantee the high level of practical  applicability and robustness of the proposed system.  This mixed system is based on the integration of  AnyLogicfor discrete-event and process flow  simulation and MATLAB/Simulink for numerical  optimization and predictive control. The simulation  is a digital analog of the restaurant ecosystem that  emulates the entire end- to-end process of the  restaurant, including procurement and preparation,  service and waste disposal, and is constantly linked  with the predictive models pegged on AI.Every  restaurant node can be represented as R j, where there  is a material flow with input Ij(t), and generated  waste W j(t) in equation (11). There are three  metrics for measuring system performance in  equations (12) & (13),    Waste Reduction Rate (WRR):     𝑊𝑅𝑅 =𝑊𝑏𝑎𝑠𝑒𝑙𝑖𝑛𝑒−𝑊𝐴𝐼 𝑊𝑏𝑎𝑠𝑒𝑙𝑖𝑛𝑒×100      (12)        Energy Efficiency Index (EEI) : The ratio between  the forecasted and the real energy consumption. Such a  digital -twin system allows adapting the de cisions  dynamically to maximize the batch size, appliance  scheduling, and storage settings to achieve the  utmost sustainability and operational accuracy.     IV.R ESULT AND DISCUSSION     The comparison of performance, as shown in Table  1, reveals that the suggested Temporal-Fusion  Neural Architecture (TFNA) is better than the  traditional models in terms of forecasting accuracy  and operational flexibility. The TFNA model  combines the temporal attention with the sequential  memory units and, therefore, can recognize complex  seasonality and external factors, including  temperature, holidays, and promotions.  Consequently, it has a Mean Absolute Percentage  Error (MAPE) of 6.5%, which is lower than the  baseline LSTM 8.9% and XGBoost 9.2% models.  Operationally, TFNA has a Root Mean Square Error  (RMSE) of 12.3 orders/day and a Mean Absolute  Error (MAE) of 7.8 units/day, which is significantly  low in comparison to other competing architectures.  The 96.2% forecast- to-procurement fill rate shows  that there is a good fit between forecasted demand  and inventory execution, which reduces shortages  and waste. Although it has a slightly higher  inference latency of 45 ms, the accuracy trade-off  compensates with its better calibration, with a Brier  score of 0.043, representing better estimates of  probabilities. In general, the TFNA model leads to  significant operational improvements and waste  reduction of more than 30 percent over baseline  models, which validates its relevance to real-time  and sustainable inventory management and  predictive decision-making in restaurants. Among the  most outstanding, the decrease in daily food waste  by 40% can be listed, which implies the  minimization of disposal expenses and the improved  use of raw materials. There are also significant  reductions in energy and water consumption 18%  and 15.2% respectively, which means that not only  is efficiency increased in the model, but also the  impact on the environment. Though  0.8% is modest growth in revenues, it is a sign of  improved satisfaction in demand and stable  alignment of the supply chain. Customer fill-rate is  one such important service metric that improved its  91.0% to 96.2% indicating that the system is  accurate in its ability to match the predicted demand  to the actual inventory situation. Sustainability Score  increased by 39.6%, totaling 78.7, which is 39.6%  higher than 56.4. Together, these findings confirm  the potential of AID-PAF to convert the  conventional restaurant management models to new  data-driven, predictive, and environmentally  sustainable ones. These data show  a definite tendency of improvement between the  years 2022 and 2024, where efficiency improved in  tiny steps as technology and process awareness  developed. Nevertheless, with the introduction of the  suggested framework, the improvements become  significantly more vivid. The current level of food  waste, amounting to 120 kg/day in 2022 and 95  kg/day in 2024, has decreased to 65 kg/day  according to AID-PAF, which is an impressive  31.6% decrease. The same trend occurs with peak- day waste and total annual waste, which decrease by  more than 31% respectively, showing the ability of  the model to control the production more accurately.  The combined financial and environmental  advantages of the framework also reduce the daily  procurement costs and the energy consumption  by18.2% and 14.6%, respectively. It is also important to note that its accuracy in forecasting has  steadily increased over the years, with 6.5% MAPE  under AID-PAF showing its strong temporal  learning. The fill-rate service efficiency increases to  96.2%, and the Sustainability Score is increasing  drastically to 78.7, improving by 26.7 points. These  results confirm the fact that the suggested system  can introduce a tangible and long-lasting change in  the operational intelligence, performance, and  environmentally friendly attitude. This graph in  Fig.4 shows the constant positive growth in waste  reduction, service efficiency, and sustainability  performance over the years, demonstrating that the  proposed AID-PAF framework contributes greatly to  the improvement of the performance of an operation  in comparison with the previous years. Smart Stock: Automating Inventory Management for  Small Warehouses with Real-Time Intelligence    Ka thyani Avuduri *  Department of Computer Science  and Engineering St.Joseph’s  College of  Engineering  Chennai -600119,  India   kathyagontu30@gmail.com    Muthu  Lakshmi  V  Department of Computer Science  and Engineering  St.Joseph’s College of Engineering  Chennai-600119, India  hodcse@stjosephs.ac.in    Sowmiya L  Department of Computer Science  and Engineering St.Joseph’s College  of Engineering  Chennai-600119, India  sowmiyajamin@gmail.com       Abstract - Small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs)  frequently rely on manual, disconnected processes for  inventory classification and stock tracking, which leads to  human error, operational delays, and inaccurate demand  forecasting. This research aims to develop an AI-powered  Smart Inventory Management System that automates stock  monitoring, improves prediction accuracy, and enhances  decision-making for replenishment. The proposed methodology  integrates QR-based product identification with a Raspberry  Pi–based edge device equipped with a camera and scanning  modules for real-time data collection. Machine learning model,  XGBoost are incorporated to classify stock levels and forecast  demand trends based on historical and live inventory data.To  ensure secure operation, the system employs role-based access  control (RBAC), QR code–based authentication, and AES-256  encryption for sensitive information. The framework further  categorizes inventory by product type, brand, and packaging  variants, enabling structured reporting and streamlined  tracking. Experimental evaluation using a custom dataset  demonstrates superior performance of the proposed predictive  model, achieving a coefficient of determination (R²) greater  than 0.95, accuracy achieved 96% outperforming traditional  approaches. The system is designed to be scalable, cost- effective, and suitable for SMEs compared to commercial  platforms like Oracle and SAP. Future enhancements include  IoT-based warehouse automation, cloud integration, and API  connectivity for e-commerce synchronization and advanced  analytics.  Keywords — XGBoost, predictive analytics, QR code,  Raspberry Pi, SMEs, machine learning  I. INTRODUCTION   Inventory management is important to supply chain  operations because it can influence how effectively a  business can operate, while minimizing costs and optimizing  customer satisfaction [1]. For small and medium-sized  enterprises (SMEs), inventory tracking and demand  forecasting are critical to their performance as they work  with limited resources and smaller profit margins [2]. This  also means that SMEs do not always have the same inventory  management tools as large-scale enterprises, which have  greater capacity for using robust information systems (i.e.,  SAP and Oracle) [3].  Much of the time, SMEs employ  manual, unrefined, and ineffective inventory management systems [4].  Traditional inventory management systems currently Due to  their heavy reliance on human input, traditional inventory  management systems employed by SMEs today result in either  too much or too little stock, incorrect inventory levels, stock  miscounts, delays in order fulfilment, and insufficient demand  projections [5]. In addition to raising the SME's operating  expenses, an ineffective supply chain makes customers  unhappy by overstocking and causing stockouts. Furthermore,  the absence of automatic real-time monitoring makes it more  difficult for SMEs to compete in a market that moves quickly  and fiercely [6].  This paper presents an AI-driven Smart Inventory  Management System designed to address the inefficiencies of  traditional approaches. The system integrates QR code –based  product identification with machine learning algorithms such  as XGBoost to perform accurate demand forecasting and sales  prediction . A Raspberry Pi–based edge platform equipped with  cameras and scanners enables automated data capture and real- time stock tracking, minimizing the need for manual  operations. To ensure secure data handling, the system  incorporates role-based access control, QR code  authentication, and AES-256 encryption. The proposed  solution is cost-effective, scalable, and highly suitable for  SMEs, offering predictive insights, improved order  fulfillment, and reduced human error  Effective inventory management is crucial for SMEs; however,  many still use manual processes which can introduce stock  discrepancies, inaccurate forecasts, and delays. Commercial  systems available today can be expensive and inconvenient for  small-scale enterprises. The project seeks to address these issues  by proposing an AI- based Smart Inventory Management System  that combines machine learning for demand forecasting and QR- based product tracking using Raspberry Pi edge devices. The  proposed system incorporates security through role-based access  and AES-256 encryption as well as automating classification,  monitoring and reporting. The combination of real-time data  capture and analytics enhances inventory visibility while  approximating a low risk of human error for stock replenishment  decisions.  II. RELATED WORK  Inventory control and demand forecasting have been the  subject of academic and industry research. Traditional  systems have been based on either manually tracking the  stock status or using primitive databases, which can have  inaccuracies in stock records and delays in updates [7]. In the  past, demand forecasting has been done using a statistical  model. Historically, statistical models such as linear  regression and ARIMA have been used to estimate demand  since they offer at least a starting point for forecasting [8].  They may, however, have difficulties, especially when  dealing with large dimensionality [9] and nonlinear patterns  [10].  Machine learning models have been used in studies to  optimize inventories in recent years. When used to forecast  stock levels, decision trees and random forests have even  performed better than traditional techniques [11].  Regretfully, those approaches perform poorly on robust or  overfitting procedures in the fast-paced retail environment  [12]. Products have also been categorized as having "low  stock" or "sufficient stock" using logistic regression [13],  although its predictive accuracy on complicated datasets is  limited [14]. Forecasts for time-series problems, like sales  and inventory levels, have improved with the development of  deep learning techniques like LSTM networks [15].  Although these model types exceed previous approaches in  terms of utilizing temporal relationships [16], their adoption  by small and medium-sized businesses is limited due to their  high data requirements and frequently enormous  computational resources  However, IoT-enabled smart inventory systems that  facilitate real-time tracking through the use of barcode  scanners [17], RFID tags, and cloud storage are already  being offered [18]. Despite their potential effectiveness,  these systems can be expensive [19] and have complicated  deployment alternatives for low-income enterprises [20]. In  order to manage supply chains in a transparent and safe way  while navigating additional overhead and complexity,  blockchain-based inventory systems have also been studied.  III. PROPOSED  SYSTEM  Due to the limitations, flaws, and disappointments of  traditional stock control, the suggested approach was  developed to improve it. The planned strategy is also  advanced, utilizing automation and artificial intelligence  through data collection and machine learning. Each stock  matter would have its own QR code. Every stock matter  would have a unique identification. By using scanning  equipment, human mistakes would be prevented during data  entry and scanning. In order to facilitate more  complex  analyses, the collected data —which included item  information, brand, price, amount in stock, and multiple  weight variants —was  then processed and imported into a  structured dataset.  The XGBoost algorithm, which is the foundation of the  system, is utilized for demand forecasting and predictive  modelling due to its high accuracy, resilience to noise, and  capacity to take non-linearity in the data into account. The  system can forecast future market behavior for demand and  identify at-risk goods for stock depletion because it has been  tr ained on historical inventory trends. When an item's stock  count hits zero or drops below a predetermined level (based  on the item or category), the system can automatically send out real-time warnings or notifications to ensure restocking  can happen on time.  Additionally, the system has a straightforward graphical  user interface that is easy for non-technical users to use and  that shows stock levels, future prediction results, and  notification alerts. It focuses on value for small and medium  business owners in a scalable and cost-effective way, unlike  current systems that mostly rely on manual work or  expensive IoT deployments.  The suggested method enhances operational performance,  decreases stockouts, saves time, and provides actionable  insights for better inventory management decisions by  fusing automated QR code scanning with machine learning- based prediction and real-time notifications          Fig.1 : Process flow of the AI-Based Smart Inventory System for  automated inventory management.    The idea behind this architecture, illustrated in Fig.1, is to  develop an automated and optimized inventory management  system using QR code scanning, IoT, cloud storage, real- time data processing, and machine learning. The architecture  consists of readily modularized modules working together to  undertake specific functionality as described below.  A. QR CODE  Each inventory item must be individually identified by the  QR scanner. Important details like product name, brand,  price, and stock count are captured by QR codes on  products. The object will be automatically captured and  transferred to the processing layer upon scanning, removing  the need for human mistakes and expediting  B. IoT Device  Real-time stock monitoring systems or middlemen are made  possible by Internet of Things goods like Raspberry Pi  boards with cameras or weight detection sensors. These  solutions communicate real-time data to the cloud database  by detecting or capturing product movement, inventory  levels, and any other designated characteristics of interest.  Automating inventory tracking in real time is the aim of the  Internet of Things.  C. Cloud Database  A centraliz ed cloud database serves as the home for the data  gathered by all of the QR scanners and Internet of Things  devices.  Scalability,  a safe way for  authorize  individuals to access data, and the capacity to sync data  across several devices are all advantages of cloud  integration. Historical data is also stored in the cloud's  central database, which can help with trend analysis and  Proceedings of the 10th International Conference on Communication and Electronics Systems (ICCES-2025) IEEE XPlore Part Number: CFP25AWO-ART; ISBN: 979-8-3315-9756-6 979-8-3315-9756-6/25/$31.00 ©2025 IEEE 1803 Authorized licensed use limited to: Mukesh Patel School of Technology & Engineering. Downloaded on July 08,2026 at 19:23:23 UTC from IEEE Xplore.  Restrictions apply. 
--- PAGE 3 ---
 predictive modeling.     D. Data collection and real time Preprocessiing   This layer collects raw data from various data sources and  standardizes it for analysis. Because data is processed in real  time, it makes it possible to get instant updates on inventory,  so it is easier to pick anomalies. For example, when the  amount for a stock item reaches zero, there will be alerts  generated to notify users immediately.  E. XGboost Algorithem   XGBoost is the core machine learning algorithm in this  system, enabling both classification and regression. It  accurately predicts product demand, consumption, and  restock levels. Its speed, scalability, and performance make  it ideal for handling large inventory datasets efficiently and  reliably in real-time applications.  F. Predictive analysis   Predictive analytics uses XGBoost outputs to forecast  demand, identify fast movers, plan replenishment, and  prevent overstock or stock-outs efficiently.  G. Dashboard  The dashboard provides administrators and managers with a  clear interface displaying stock levels, demand forecasts, and  real-time alerts, enabling quick decision-making and efficient  monitoring of overall inventory performance.  IV. EXPERIMENTAL  SETUP  AND  RESULTS ANALYSIS    A. Dataset description  The unique dataset utiliz ed in this study was created  specifically to make inventory management in retail settings  easier. The 1000 records and 6 attributes in the collection  include pertinent information required for intelligent stock  and demand forecast monitoring. The qualities are as  follows:  • QR Code: a unique code assigned to each product to  expedite scanning and retrieval when adding stock and  for billing.  • Product_Name: the name of the product (Milk, Biscuits,  Cheese) and therefore, the category of goods stored  under of inventory.  • Price: the price of the product in terms of money, which is  a necessary characteristic as it informs demand and  turnover.  •  Brand: the manufacturer or company the product is  associated, (Nestle, Britannia, Dabur), which is  important as it informs consumer preferences and other  competitive goods.  •  Count: the count of available stock of that product in the  inventory, the main source of addressing low-stock or  out-of-stock status.  • Quantity: the weight or volume of the product content in  packaging, expressed in numbers (250, 500, 1000),  which is important for purchase frequency and demand.    The dataset reflects real inventory behavior by  incorporating price, brand, and packaging variations  that affect demand. Frequently used items have higher  stock levels than luxury goods. Multiple brands per  category enable demand analysis. The data trains  XGBoost models for forecasting, shortage detection,  and real-time zero-stock alerts.Algorithm 1: Smart Inventory Prediction and  Management System by using XGBoost.
